"Son of noble family, in the same way, when no buddha is present in this buddha field, the Sahā world, a world system of the five degenerations, all the sentient beings who live there are completely bound by the afflictions latent in their own minds and are harassed by inner enemies and outer enemies. Afflicted by the afflictions, blinded by delusion, and unhappy, they cling and attach themselves to eternalism and annihilationism, generate measureless attachment, and quarrel and contend with one another. Despoiling and reviling one another, they give rise to attachment, aversion and delusion; they speak with guile and deceit, and carry to completion the courses of action of the ten nonvirtues.

"Those clinging and attached people, tormented in this world system of afflictions by the many kinds of disease of the afflictions, are blind to the eye of the true Dharma. Though they are burned and agitated by aversion and anger, they do not attend in the least to the true Dharma of reality. Having completely abandoned the taste of the true Dharma, they despoil and revile those who do good. They become bereft and destitute of the ease and happiness they had before; constantly wrapped in and entangled by many kinds of nets of affliction, they go for refuge to the six non-Buddhist (tīrthika) teachers, turn away from the noble path, and become bent upon the three lower realms.

"At that time, in that field, all the bodhisattvas, the great beings, who in the past had made offerings to and served countless buddhas, who had entered the ocean of the buddhas' qualities, and who abide in the sphere of activity of the buddhas of the past, gathered and assembled, came before me, and with one voice said this: 'In past times you applied yourself earnestly to measureless giving, guarded moral discipline perfectly, and with diligence of the utmost exertion practiced what is extremely hard to practice. You have cultivated all of it to perfection.'

"Thus, adorned with such accumulations of merit and wisdom, with wisdom, skill in means, great compassion and the rest, I became a treasury of great qualities. I gained full mastery of all samādhis and dhāraṇīs, and became an ocean in which the qualities of all the grounds are complete. I was without guile or deceit; my body was very tall and perfectly endowed with the major and minor marks; I was possessed of gentle forbearance and supremely beautiful. Following no one else's lead, in cultivating the path of awakening I brought the whole ocean of wisdom to completion. I came to possess the supreme and excellent complexion, became the great teacher of all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and became the ally of all beings who are terrified of saṃsāra. I was adorned with measureless qualities such as loving-kindness and great compassion. I was the true son of the blessed ones Krakucchanda, Kanakamuni, Kāśyapa and the others, the one who attains full awakening in this Fortunate Eon, the chief among all bodhisattvas.

"Fragrant with qualities of many kinds, I had bathed in the pure water of calm abiding and insight and put on the garments of shame and modesty. I wore the pure dharmadhātu as the jewel in my topknot and wore a diadem of garlands of flowers, the vast sphere of activity of all the buddhas, bound with the thread of liberation. I was adorned besides with the jewels of qualities such as the wisdom of omniscience and the forbearance regarding the non-arising of phenomena. I wore the precious sandals of great loving-kindness, compassion, joy and equanimity. I was shaded by the parasol that brings to completion the causes of the noble state through the three kinds of conduct that cover the three realms. The seat of the vajra-like samādhi, upheld by the buddhas of the past, by the gods and by the sages, was set out. I sat upon the four applications of mindfulness, upheld with reverence by all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Seated upon the seat spread by all the buddhas of the past, I fully awakened into unsurpassed awakening.

"Having become the wisdom of omniscience, I turned the wheel of Dharma so that the lineage of the Three Jewels would not be cut off. Beating the great drum of the Dharma, I proclaimed the sound of the Dharma throughout the three realms, and by means of the four noble truths I liberated all the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, rākṣasas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, kumbhāṇḍas, bhūtas, piśācas, pūtanas, kaṭapūtanas, humans, nonhumans and the rest. The wheel of Dharma with twelve aspects, which no one in the world, whether śramaṇa or brahmin, god, māra or Brahmā, human or nonhuman, had ever turned, that wheel which no one had turned before, I turned thoroughly in three ways, so that countless gods and humans in the world might attain the great, supreme and sacred aim of benefit and happiness.

"Son of noble family, I possess such a single buddha wheel. By the power of that wheel I know directly, exactly as they truly are, this world and the world beyond, what is possible and what is impossible, and so I have attained fearlessness. I dwell at ease, and having thoroughly subdued all non-Buddhists of false doctrine, together with the gods and māras, I turn the wheel of the great Brahmā. Because I have perfectly accomplished the conduct of the great Brahmā, in this evil world system of the five degenerations I proclaim the lion's roar in the midst of the assembly and pacify the deeds of immediate retribution of all sentient beings. In the same way, I clear away everything that belongs to the roots of nonvirtue and pacify even the streams of affliction of all beings, which are as hard as a vajra. I establish them in the sacred fruit of liberation in which all defilements are exhausted, setting each in happiness according to his inclination, and those beings who have the power I also establish in the three vehicles and make irreversible.

"Son of noble family, it is like this. As soon as a great crowned kṣatriya king first ascends the throne and takes up the rule of the kingdom, he looks closely and reflects on the laws and statutes of all the kings of the past, future and present. Having thus thoroughly understood the wheel of activity of all kings, he also knows how causes ripen into results. According to what each person is suited for, he gives them the corresponding means and sets them to work, and so he has the wise and the simple who live in that land carry out three kinds of wheel of activity. By the power of that wheel of activity, all the beings who live there enjoy for a long time many kinds of pleasing necessities of life, their happiness and well-being increase and grow, and all hostile enemies are subdued.

"What are the three kinds of wheel of activity? They are these. By establishing the wheel of activity of governing the kingdom, he has those who are skilled trained in the strategies of war and practiced in martial arts, so that they subdue hostile enemies and protect the many people. By establishing the wheel of activity of fields and dwellings, he has people build houses and carry out the work of the fields with tools, and by making them comfortable he ensures they do not lack food and drink. By establishing the wheel of activity of seeking wealth and precious things, he has people learn trade and many kinds of crafts, so that they have many kinds of necessities of life to use as they wish, and their happiness and well-being increase.

"Son of noble family, when a great crowned kṣatriya king possesses these three royal wheels, by the power of these wheels his land becomes very happy and prosperous; hostile enemies and bad associates are subdued; and because he is skilled in diligently guarding his person, his life too becomes long.

"Son of noble family, in the same way, when the Tathāgata has first fully awakened and attained unsurpassed wisdom, he reflects closely on the Dharma eye of all the buddhas of the past, future and present. Through this close examination he fully knows the causes and results of actions and of phenomena, and he establishes three kinds of wheel of activity for all the beings who are to be trained. By the power of that wheel of activity, the lineage of the Three Jewels and the Dharma eye long endure; the unsurpassed true Dharma spreads widely everywhere; all beings constantly attain great happiness, from rebirth as gods in the higher realms up to passing into nirvāṇa; no non-Buddhist (tīrthika) of false doctrine can withstand it; and by means of the eye of the true Dharma all opponents are subdued in accordance with the Dharma.

"Son of noble family, what then are the three kinds of wheel of activity? They are these three: establishing the wheel of activity of cultivating samādhi, establishing the wheel of activity of becoming practiced in recitation, and establishing the wheel of activity of doing meritorious deeds.

"Son of noble family, what is the Tathāgata's wheel of activity of cultivating samādhi? There are ten kinds of samādhi. What are the ten? When this body together with its consciousness is examined exactly as it is, it is the six objects, the cause of clinging and attachment to self and mine, the field of karma, obscured by ignorance and moistened by the water of craving; it exists without autonomy, under the power of others, and so it depends on many conditions. One examines it in this way in order to stop the three streams: of karma, of affliction and of suffering.

"What is the stream of karma? It is whatever formations sentient beings actively engage in. Because becoming arises from the causes of ignorance and craving, that is called the stream of affliction. Through the power of the afflictions, from the coming together of the many causes and conditions of consciousness, name and form arise. From the coming together of the many causes and conditions of name and form, the six sense sources arise. From the coming together of the many causes and conditions of the six sense sources there arise in sequence contact, feeling, birth in a further existence, aging, death and the rest: this is called the stream of suffering. Thus these three streams arise in the field of karma through the cause of ignorance and the moisture of craving.

"So, in order to dry up these three, which arise in the field of karma through the cause of ignorance and the moisture of craving, one realizes that the five appropriated aggregates are impermanent, suffering, selfless, inert, without movement, like an illusion, like a mirage, like the moon in water, like a dream, empty, nothing whatever, signless, wishless, unmade, not come into being, unborn, formless, peaceful, isolated, and without any ground for origination or birth. By thus examining the five appropriated aggregates closely, one inclines toward the forbearance that accords with emptiness, the forbearance that accords with signlessness, and the forbearance that accords with wishlessness.

"Furthermore, in order to examine the five appropriated aggregates further, through skill in means one also observes the breath going in and out, and cultivates mindfulness of the in-breath and the out-breath. How then does one, through the power of mindfulness, observe the breath going in and out exactly as it is? Exactly as it is, one observes counting, following, calm abiding, insight, engagement and purification.

"Of these, counting performs two functions: by serving as a support, it subdues conceptual thought and analysis, and it makes one able to apprehend the characteristic of the breath going in and out. Following also performs two functions: relying on deliverance, one becomes free of conceptual thought and analysis, and one becomes skilled in the characteristic of the movement of the breath going in and out. Calm abiding also performs two functions: it manifestly shows the breath going in and out diminishing, and it makes one able to abide in the supreme samādhi.

Insight also performs two functions: it manifestly shows the breath going in and out coming to an end, and it abides in the discernment, one by one, of mind and the mental factors. Engagement also performs two functions: one becomes skilled in the practice of abandoning the aggregates of clinging, and one applies oneself to entering the noble ground. Purification also performs two functions to be accomplished: the abandoning of thorough affliction, and the complete purification of view. In this way, by cultivating the examination of the breath going in and out through these six kinds of practice, one enters into conformity with the realization of the five aggregates of clinging.

Why is that? The nature of the breath going in and out is the aggregate of clinging that is form. Taking in and experiencing the breath going in and out is the aggregate of clinging that is feeling. Apprehending the characteristic of the breath going in and out is the aggregate of clinging that is perception. Making the breath going in and out manifest is the aggregate of clinging that is formations. Distinguishing the breath going in and out is the aggregate of clinging that is consciousness. The five aggregates of clinging, explained in this way, are individual, distinct and dissimilar; they are not new and not old; they have no location; they are not without accumulation; and they are inexpressible.

By thoroughly realizing the five aggregates of clinging in this way, one completely abandons the three kinds of formations. If one thus reaches the culmination of investigating the complete exhaustion of the three kinds of formations, one reaches the culmination of understanding the body together with consciousness, and the six objects. Clinging to self and to what is one's own, karma, ignorance, the cause of existence, the field, and the moisture that envelops it are also all exhausted. Likewise, the cultivation of the four applications of mindfulness becomes complete, and so on up to the noble eightfold path, whose cultivation also becomes complete. Likewise, up to the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha, cultivation also becomes complete. Likewise, up to the wisdom of omniscience, the forbearance of the nonarising of phenomena, the samādhi of the Heroic March (śūraṃgama) and so on, cultivation becomes complete.

Likewise, one who has entered the meditative concentrations by holding to going and coming with mindfulness is called a being who abides in the true Dharma, the ultimate. This is called cultivating meditative concentration correctly, just as it is. It is called rightly honoring the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the three times. Such a one is called a child arisen from the heart of all the buddhas, born from the speech of them all, accomplished by the Dharma, and tamed by the Dharma.

Some bodhisattvas who have meditated in this way, even if they gradually fall back, become arhats whose defilements are exhausted and come to possess the six superknowledges. Some bodhisattvas who have meditated in this way rise higher and higher, perfect all good qualities, and, having become bodhisattva great beings, perfect everything up to the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha and the wisdom of omniscience; before long they will fully awaken into unsurpassed awakening.

Son of noble family, when with wisdom I examined the Dharma of the buddhas of the three times concerning so many kinds of karma, phenomena, feelings, causes, results and karmic maturations, I established for sentient beings the ten wheels of the action of cultivating samādhi, and set them to meditation. Son of noble family, these are called the wheel of the Tathāgata's action of cultivating samādhi.

Son of noble family, what, then, is the wheel of the Tathāgata's action of training in recitation? Monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen, sons of noble family or daughters of noble family whose roots of virtue are small and thin, who abide in conventional truth, and whose faculties are not fully matured, I set to training in recitation, so that in the evening and at dawn they exert themselves with intense diligence, free of laziness. Those sentient beings who seek unsurpassed wisdom I set in the utterly pure, white Great Vehicle, so that they themselves read and cause others to read, themselves recite and cause others to recite, themselves retain and cause others to retain, themselves become trained in the Great Vehicle and cause others to become trained, and so that they strive to clear away the afflictions of themselves and others, to attain unsurpassed omniscient wisdom, to pacify the suffering of all sentient beings, and to dwell in the great city of fearlessness. Those sentient beings who aspire to the vehicle of the pratyekabuddhas I set in the teaching of dependent origination and had them train in its recitation. Those sentient beings who aspire to the vehicle of the śrāvakas I had train in the recitation of the scriptures of a hundred thousand verses, the Vinaya of a hundred thousand verses, the Abhidharma and the Abhidharma discourses of a hundred thousand verses, and so on. That, son of noble family, is called the wheel of the Tathāgata's action of training in recitation.

Son of noble family, what, then, is the wheel of the Tathāgata's action of making merit? Those sentient beings who are of dull faculties, who have not developed roots of virtue, who are weak in wisdom and lazy, whose mindfulness has lapsed, who are attached and clinging to every kind of requisite for living, and who forsake their spiritual friends, I set to the work of making merit: that is, doing service for the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha, and doing service for preceptors and masters. Son of noble family, that is called the wheel of the Tathāgata's action of accomplishing merit.

Son of noble family, in this way a buddha possesses this second wheel. By the power of that wheel, with the unsurpassed wisdom of the karma of the three times, he knows correctly, just as it is, the karma, the phenomena, the feelings, the causes and the full ripening of the results of all sentient beings. Having established the three kinds of wheels of action according to whatever will tame each one, he places all sentient beings who have become fit vessels happily in benefit, in well-being, and in fearlessness. Through fearlessness he tames all the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras, and turns the wheel of the great Brahmā; and because the conduct of the great Brahmā is perfected, he clearly knows, correctly and just as it is, all the causes and results of sentient beings.

Son of noble family, it is like this. A great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, possesses an intelligence skilled in means. To ascetics, brahmins, warriors, merchants, commoners and the like who have various kinds of good qualities, who are learned, heroic, skilled in crafts or of great prowess, and to any beings who are wealthy and endowed with good qualities, possessed of an intelligence skilled in means, brave and bold, steadfast, never turning back, and adorned with many kinds of merit, the great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, gives, as befits and suits what each of them possesses, jewels, wealth and riches, fields and pastures, houses, servants, laborers and so on, as is appropriate. To the people living in that land whose good qualities and knowledge are weak, and who have done little work, the great king anointed on the crown of his head into kingship also gives a share, as befits and suits them. But those living in that land who have no merit and little diligence, who are very lazy, careless, of lapsed mindfulness, without a compassionate mind, ungrateful, who do not regard with fear the sufferings of the next world, who are sunk in the mire of the household, and who commit many misdeeds, the great king punishes with various kinds of penalties, as suits and befits them. Some he rebukes and scolds with harsh words. From some he seizes their jewels, wealth, goods and the like. From some he seizes even their requisites for living. Some he flogs with the whip and throws into prison. Of some he cuts off the limbs and extremities. Of some he cuts off even the head and body. With these and various other kinds of punishment he punishes them. Son of noble family, in this way a great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, possesses this third royal wheel. By the power of that wheel his land is exceedingly happy and prosperous; he subdues all opposing enemies and bad companions, and, being skilled in the care of guarding his person, his life too becomes long.

Son of noble family, in the same way the Tathāgata too possesses a wisdom supremely skilled concerning the faculties. Those of the assembly who have grown far from the accumulations of merit and wisdom and from skillful means and wisdom, who have completely abandoned generosity, discipline and peace, and whose minds have become disturbed because their mindfulness has lapsed, come to where I am and come to me for refuge. Because I fully know their faculties, faith, latent tendencies and inclinations, I teach them, according to whatever will tame each one, the Dharma of the means of punishment, the Vinaya and so on. Those sentient beings who have come under the sway of pride and do not uphold the many precepts, I have perform these things, so that the true Dharma of the teaching may remain for a long time. Some I punish by a pledge that serves as a reminder. Some I punish by threatening with harsh words, by reproach and by scolding. Some I punish by brief separation and by bringing them to shame. Some I punish by their not being bowed to, not being spoken to, and not sharing in gains. Some are dealt with by something like the spreading of grass, and some are punished with penalties such as expulsion. In this way, because I know fully the faculties, faith, latent tendencies and inclinations of those persons, I punish them as suits and befits them. Moreover, in order to destroy the accumulated mass of the darkness of meaninglessness, to dry up the river of the afflictions, and to attain everything from birth as a god in the higher realms up to the bliss of nirvāṇa, I have taught the Dharma of punishment, in whatever way is fitting, in order to tame persons who engage in non-virtue. In many sūtras too, the Dharma of punishing those who engage in non-virtue, in whatever way is fitting for each, is explained at length.

With supreme and sacred wisdom I also fully know those sentient beings who possess superior intention and completely pure faith and inclination. According to whatever will tame each one, I teach them the many kinds of distinctions on the side of virtue, cause them to train and become habituated, bring to completion everything up to all their roots of virtue, and lead them into the great city of fearlessness.

Son of noble family, in this way I possess this third wheel of a buddha. By the power of this wheel, because I know all the various faculties, the faith, the latent tendencies, the inclinations, the intentions, the karma and the experiences of all sentient beings, I place them happily in fearlessness, according to whatever will tame each one, by means of benefit and well-being. Through fearlessness I claim the place of the great sage, fully turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all the non-Buddhists who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras, and roar the lion's roar in the midst of a great assembly.

Son of noble family, it is like this. A great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, learns of however many persons in his land have gone for refuge to worldly gods and to the various kinds of non-Buddhists, who have faith in what is false, hold false views, train in what is false, are fixated on false ascetic observances, and, attached to false omens of good and bad, have come to suffer from many kinds of pointless suffering. Having learned of them, that great king gathers them together again and again, and having taught them at length by the custom of the true Dharma of the former kings, he decrees: "Give up false views and false faith, and diligently practice the training of the Dharma customs of the former kings!" Thus all the sentient beings living in that land, of one engagement, one orientation, one intention and one aspiration, all in harmony, abide in accordance with the true Dharma of the former kings; and because they listen to his command and follow it, all who live there do in harmony whatever ought to be done. The great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, also constantly gathers with his ministers, holds feasts and enjoys himself; delighting in joyful amusement and in harmony of counsel, he carries out the affairs of the king. Son of noble family, in this way a great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, possesses this fourth royal wheel. By the power of that wheel his land is exceedingly happy and prosperous; he subdues all opposing enemies and bad companions, and, being skilled in the care of guarding his person, his life too becomes long.

Son of noble family, in just the same way the Tathāgata too possesses the wisdom that knows inclinations with supreme skill in means. The Tathāgata knows and sees that in the world those inclined in many ways toward what is false, who hold false views, have false intentions, are devoted to false teachings and engage in false actions, experience immeasurable suffering through that cause and that condition. Having known and seen this, he constantly gathers great assemblies again and again, and opens up and clearly teaches at length the Dharma of causes and results: the cause and the result of continuing the lineage of the Three Jewels of the buddhas, the blessed ones, who have passed in the past, the six perfections, the cause of the yogin, the three vows and so on; and thus he liberates the great assemblies. Those with distorted views enter right view and the right path of action of the ten virtues. Sentient beings constantly engage in the Dharma in harmony; by skillful means he guides them to practice the Dharma and sets them to meditation, and so makes cause and result continue without interruption. Having had sentient beings, in harmony with the four assemblies, apply themselves to the distinctions of virtuous conduct, they take delight, as though at play, in the four applications of mindfulness, in samādhi, in the teaching of the wisdom of liberation, and in the limbs of the path. In order that the teaching of the noble ones may long remain, and that the lineage of the Three Jewels may not be interrupted, he lets them delight freely in the four right abandonments, the four bases of miraculous power, the five faculties, the five powers, the seven limbs of awakening, the noble eightfold path, other samādhis besides, the teaching of the wisdom of liberation, and the many kinds of distinctions of the factors of awakening.

Son of noble family, in this way I possess this fourth wheel of a buddha. By the power of that wheel, because I know whatever each ordinary being is devoted to, whatever each is inclined toward, and whatever each delights in, I place them happily in fearlessness, according to whatever will tame each one, by means of benefit and well-being. Through fearlessness I dwell in the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all the non-Buddhists who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras, and roar the lion's roar in the midst of a great assembly." So he spoke.

"Son of noble family, it is like this. Countless sentient beings, whether living in his own land or in the land of another, are attached to wealth and forms and know no weariness of them, and are attached to and covet the wealth and forms of others. To guard against this, a great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, surrounds the villages, towns, hamlets and even the royal palace with retinues and walls, and sets guards over everything down to the mynah birds and parrots, so that nothing is lost or ruined. Son of noble family, in this way a great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, possesses this fifth royal wheel. By the power of that wheel his land is exceedingly happy and prosperous; he subdues all opposing enemies and bad companions, and, being skilled in the care of guarding his person, his life too becomes long.

Son of noble family, in the same way the Tathāgata too possesses the wisdom that knows intrinsic natures with supreme skill in means. He fully knows the wicked and savage Māra, the ninety-five kinds of non-Buddhists, and besides them the hosts of Māra and the countless beings deceived by non-Buddhists. Sentient beings who are fixated on their own wealth and forms are also attached to the wealth and forms of others, and, coveting them, seek after them. Giving rise to jealousy toward me and my assembly, in order to kill me they put poison in my alms food, spread a seat over a fire pit, hurled things down from above, let loose a maddened elephant, drew swords and pursued me, and scattered earth and filth. With many kinds of evil words they also reviled and slandered me, accusing me, saying, "He indulges in unchaste conduct," labeling me, "He is a eunuch," saying, "He is not human," and saying, "He is a conjurer." Knowing that, giving rise to jealousy toward my śrāvaka disciples too, through that cause and that condition they slander and revile them in many ways, the Tathāgata, being skilled in guarding the six faculties, abiding in the four abodes of Brahmā, and having come to possess the four discriminating knowledges, also taught the śrāvakas the Dharma sūtras that set forth the completely pure three doors of liberation.

I now know, correctly and just as it is, every nature, worldly and beyond the world. Having thus known all the many different natures of sentient beings, he acts for their benefit and well-being, according to whatever will tame each one. Son of noble family, in this way I possess this fifth wheel of a buddha. By the power of that wheel, with the wisdom that knows natures, worldly and beyond the world, I know individually all the immeasurable and various natures of sentient beings, and so, according to whatever will tame each one, I place them happily in fearlessness by means of benefit and well-being. Through fearlessness I dwell in the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all the non-Buddhists who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras, and fully roar the lion's roar in the midst of a great assembly.

Son of noble family, it is like this. In order to keep his cities, towns, hamlets and royal palace firmly guarded, a great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, surrounds them with moats, and so on as in the passage before, setting guards over everything down to the mynah birds and parrots. In the palace, entirely surrounded by his retinue, his queens and daughters, he sports in the five sense pleasures and, heedless among the many kinds of enjoyments, indulges his six faculties at ease. Son of noble family, in this way that great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, possesses this sixth royal wheel. By the power of that wheel his land is exceedingly happy and prosperous; he subdues all opposing enemies and bad companions, and, being skilled in the care of guarding his person, his life too becomes long.

Son of noble family, in just the same way the Tathāgata too, having set the bodhisattva great beings and all the great śrāvakas within the firm city of the teaching taught by the noble ones, thereafter enters into absorption, from absorption in the supreme and sacred meditative concentration up to absorption in the four meditative concentrations; enters into absorption from the samādhi of the sphere of infinite space, and likewise at length up to the samādhi of the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception; and likewise enters into absorption up to the samādhi that is the domain of all the buddhas. Having entered those samādhis, I see countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, bhūtas, piśācas, pūtanas, kaṭapūtanas and others, who harm and injure sentient beings, who have no loving-kindness or compassion in their minds, and who have no fear of the sufferings of the next world. When they see me entered into the samādhi that is the domain of all the buddhas, they all give rise to a mind of great joy toward me, and with completely pure intention they give rise to supreme and sacred joy and faith in the Three Jewels. With reverence and with respect, struck with wonder, they confess every fault individually and vow to cut them off completely. Through that cause and that condition, in a single instant, a mere moment, all their immeasurable and incalculable obscurations of affliction, obscurations of karma and obscurations of phenomena are purified. They complete all the immeasurable and incalculable accumulations of merit and accumulations of wisdom, turn their backs on saṃsāra and turn toward nirvāṇa, and protect and uphold the unsurpassed true Dharma of the Tathāgata.

Son of noble family, in this way I too possess this sixth wheel of a buddha. By the power of that wheel the Tathāgata enters with completely pure wisdom into countless hundreds of thousands of meditative concentrations, samādhis, liberations, absorptions and states of equipoise, clears away the countless afflictions of sentient beings, and, according to whatever will tame each one, places them happily in fearlessness by means of benefit and well-being. Through fearlessness I claim the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all the non-Buddhists who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras, and roar the lion's roar in the midst of a great assembly." So he spoke.

"Son of noble family, it is like this. A great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, together with his ministers and his fourfold army, tours and inspects all the cities, towns, hamlets, mountains, regions, valleys, side valleys, pleasure groves, fields, pastures, rivers, ponds, lakes, springs, remote pastures, forests and so on that are under his rule. In whatever places in that land are beset by danger and doubt, or are perilous and unfit to be approached, or where enemies from outside raid and do harm, the great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, assesses them by his own experience and puts them right in every way. He applies himself to making them secure, guards them well and makes them firm, so that those regions are made even and workable, and leaves no place where foreign enemies and bad companions can watch for an opening and raid. In order to remove fear and anxiety, he protects all the sentient beings living in that land, so that they are free from the suffering of unease and are exceedingly happy and prosperous. Son of noble family, in this way a great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, possesses that seventh royal wheel. By the power of that wheel his land is exceedingly happy and prosperous; he subdues all opposing enemies and bad companions, and, being skilled in the care of guarding his person, his life too becomes long.

Son of noble family, in just the same way the Tathāgata too, with the eye of a buddha, fully knows, correctly and just as it is, whatever mentality of attachment, aversion or delusion each sentient being has. Correctly and just as it is, he also fully knows the many kinds of disorders of the afflictions.

Because the Tathāgata knows this, with immeasurable, most strenuous diligence and by the force of his skill in means, he gives, according to whatever will tame each one, the sacred medicine of accomplishing the many kinds of samādhi, and so has sentient beings apply themselves with care and diligence to curing the disease of the afflictions. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease of the afflictions is fit to be calmed by contemplating the unattractive, he has that one cultivate the medicine of contemplating the unattractive. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease of the afflictions is fit to be calmed by applying oneself to the abodes of Brahmā, he has that one cultivate the medicine of the abodes of Brahmā. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease of the afflictions is fit to be cured by meditating on dependent origination, he has that one cultivate the medicine of dependent origination. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease is fit to be calmed by attending to the counting of the breath, he has that one cultivate the medicine of attending to the counting of the breath. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease of the afflictions is fit to be calmed by meditating on the three doors of liberation, he has that one cultivate the medicine of the three doors of liberation. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease of the afflictions is fit to be calmed by cultivating meditative concentration, he has that one cultivate the medicine of meditative concentration. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease of the afflictions is fit to be calmed by cultivating the formless meditative concentrations, he has that one cultivate the formless meditative concentrations. When there is a sentient being for whom the disease of the afflictions is fit to be calmed by cultivating up to the samādhi of the Heroic March (śūraṃgama), he has that one cultivate the medicine of the samādhi of the Heroic March.

Therefore the Tathāgata makes such a gift of the medicine of the Dharma to sentient beings. He does not let any sentient beings who have become fit vessels fall under the power of the four māras. He does not let any sentient beings who have become fit vessels turn their backs on the vehicle of gods and humans and sink toward the lower realms. He does not let the unsurpassed medicine of the Dharma of the Tathāgata and the lineage of the Three Jewels swiftly disappear. In this way the Tathāgata makes such a gift of the medicine of the Dharma to sentient beings.

Son of noble family, the Tathāgata possesses such a seventh wheel of a buddha. By the power of that wheel, through the conduct of unsurpassed, all-pervading wisdom, he gives many kinds of medicine of the Dharma to all sentient beings; when they are set to cultivating it, all the diseases of the afflictions are calmed, and they come to abide happily in fearlessness. Through fearlessness I dwell in the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all the non-Buddhists who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras, and roar the lion's roar in the midst of a great assembly.

Son of noble family, it is like this. A great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, thinks, "Let me set my mindfulness on all things, my own lineage and that of others, my first birth, the play of my youth and so on." That is, he thinks back on and recalls such things as these: the place where he was first born; being bathed; being taken onto a lap and given breast milk; the massaging of his body and limbs; playing joyfully; playing in earth and sand; playing heedlessly in many ways with his companions; training in skills; doing various other kinds of activities; traveling and going to the lands of other kings; carrying out royal duties; performing the work of a king; acting as regent; having gained the kingdom and come into power, experiencing great and happy enjoyments; and being renowned in all directions with great fame. Having recalled these, he establishes the true Dharma customs of the former kings, sustains and cares for all the sentient beings living there, holds his own realm, and does no harm to others. Son of noble family, in this way that great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, possesses this eighth royal wheel. By the power of that wheel his land is exceedingly happy and prosperous; he subdues all opposing enemies and bad companions, and, being skilled in the care of guarding his person, his life too becomes long.

Son of noble family, in just the same way the Tathāgata too, when dwelling in the midst of the assembly, recalls everything that he and others did in countless former births. That is, he recalls everything done in one birth, in two births, in three births, and so on up to countless hundreds of thousands of births. Likewise he recalls the formation of eons, and recalls too the destruction of eons. He recalls everything up to countless formations of eons and countless destructions of eons: "In past times this one lived in such-and-such a place; the family was called this, the name was this, the clan was this; this one ate this food and experienced these pleasures and pains; the life span was this long; this one lived for this long. At the end of that life, this one died in such-and-such a place and was born in these places; dying in these places, this one was born in such-and-such a place." Because he recalls all these and other immeasurable and incalculable things, he teaches the true Dharma in accordance with the individual faculties of sentient beings, and acts for their benefit and well-being.

Son of noble family, in this way I possess this eighth wheel of a buddha. By the power of that wheel I place countless sentient beings happily in fearlessness by means of benefit and well-being. Through fearlessness I dwell in the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all who speak falsely, the non-Buddhists and others, together with the gods and māras, and roar the lion's roar in the midst of a great assembly.

Son of noble family, it is like this. A great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, just as he has in mind, thoroughly examines the family, the clan, whatever skills they have, whatever work they do, where they will be born after dying from here, and all the differing distinctions of good and bad causes and results, of the sentient beings living within his domain.

He knows which sentient beings, born into which households, are bold and heroic, and which are weak and timid; which, when trained in a craft, become adept at it, and which do not; what work each one does; who does virtue and who does evil; who is rich and powerful and who is poor and wretched; who is beautiful and who is not beautiful, and so forth. Knowing all this, he knows everything about them right up until they pass away. He fully understands which of them die before their karma is exhausted and which die when their karma is exhausted; which are executed for breaking the king's law; which are killed by enemies; which die from being flogged with whips; which die from being thrown into prison; which die for the sake of training in a craft; which die on the battlefield against hostile foes; which die through quarrels and fighting; which die for the sake of guarding their wealth; which die under the sway of desire; which die from anger and holding grudges; which die of exhaustion; which die of hunger; which die burdened with faults and wrongdoing; which die blameless; which die worn out with old age; which die in the prime of life; which die in youth and childhood; which die having done many kinds of virtuous actions; and which die having done many kinds of non-virtuous actions. He knows which sentient beings, when they die, will be born in the higher realms because they practiced virtue, and he fully understands which sentient beings, when they pass on, will be born in the lower realms because they practiced non-virtue.

Having thus fully understood all these matters, he thinks, "I too must apply myself diligently and strive hard to practice virtue of body, virtue of speech, and virtue of mind." He thinks, "By all sorts of means I must make myself accustomed to giving, and become peaceful and disciplined, so that when I pass on I may be born in a good state and not fall into the lower realms."

So the great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, having reflected on such matters, exerts himself greatly, with diligence of the utmost effort, in practicing virtue with all three: body, speech, and mind. He constantly practices giving, namely: food, drink, clothing, oxen, horses, vehicles for riding, bedding, medicine for the sick, houses, butter lamps and lanterns, and every other necessity of life besides; servants and laborers; and further, jewels and wealth of every kind; his head, his eyes, his hands and his feet, even up to his body and his life. All of this he gives away without stinginess. He completely abandons everything from the taking of life, through taking what is not given, sexual misconduct, lying, harsh speech, idle talk, slander, attachment and aversion, up to wrong view.

Through that cause and that condition, the great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, obtains ten kinds of distinguished qualities. What are the ten? They are these. He has great renown. He has wealth and jewels. He has excellent bodily marks. He has a large retinue. He is free of discomfort and disease. His retinue, kinsmen, and friends are all of sharp wisdom and great learning. He relies on and honors the genuine and those of genuine conduct. He is widely renowned, his fair fame spreading throughout the ten directions. He is constantly protected by gods endowed with great splendor and power. And when he passes on, he is born as a god in the higher realms and always dwells in good states, possessed of supremely blissful enjoyments.

Son of noble family, the great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, thus possesses this ninth wheel. By the power of possessing that wheel, his domain is happy and prosperous, and, having subdued all hostile foes and evil companions and being skilled in the care that protects his person, his life too becomes long.

Son of noble family, in just the same way, the Tathāgata too knows exactly as it really is everything concerning the birth, death, and passing on of all sentient beings. He fully knows, exactly as it really is, which sentient beings engage in non-virtuous conduct of body, which sentient beings engage in non-virtuous conduct of speech, and which sentient beings engage in non-virtuous conduct of mind, disparage the noble ones, and hold wrong views, and so, by the power of wrong view, fall into the lower realms when they pass on: which of them fall into the hells, which are born in the animal realm, and which are born as hungry ghosts. He fully knows which sentient beings engage in virtuous conduct of body, which engage in virtuous conduct of speech, and which engage in virtuous conduct of mind, do not disparage the noble ones, and hold right view, and so, through the condition of right view, when they pass on are born as gods in the higher realms, are born as humans, or come to the exhaustion of the defilements.

Knowing thus exactly as it really is, the Tathāgata generates loving-kindness and great compassion for those sentient beings, and with diligence of the utmost effort he displays three kinds of miracle. He turns those beings toward the Dharma and Vinaya taught by the Buddha, and establishes them in mundane and supramundane faith. What are the three? They are the miracle of the higher knowledges, the miracle of teaching the Dharma, and the miracle of knowing the minds of others. Having urged those beings on with these three kinds of miracle, he establishes them in the teaching, and having brought them to attain mundane and supramundane faith, he liberates all sentient beings who wander in cyclic existence.

Son of noble family, I thus possess this ninth wheel of a buddha. By the power of that wheel, for the sake of the benefit and happiness of immeasurable sentient beings, I establish them in the happiness of fearlessness. Being fearless, I dwell in the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all the non-Buddhists who hold wrong views, together with the gods and māras, and roar the lion's roar in the midst of the assembly.

Son of noble family, it is like this. In order to remove the many kinds of bodily illness of the immeasurable sentient beings of the four continents, a great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, sets aside the affairs of state, washes his body with scented water, puts on clean new garments, and sits with his body upright. Bringing to mind loving-kindness, compassion, and an impartial attitude toward all sentient beings, and in order to free them from every illness, he makes offerings to all the mighty and splendid gods with flowers, incense, music of many kinds, and a great many other offering articles of every sort.

At that time all of them, from the lords of the gods and the lords of the nāgas up to the lords of the mahoragas, come to know of this and say to one another, "This great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, is endowed with good qualities. Because his splendor is great, he is worthy to be a wheel-turning monarch who rules over the four continents. Let us go to him and empower him as king over the four continents. We go so that sentient beings may be free of illness and happy." Having said this, all of them, from the lords of the gods up to the lords of the mahoragas, came and empowered that great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, as a wheel-turning monarch. He came to possess the seven precious things and to rule over the four continents, and he had a thousand sons, all of them heroic and bold, handsome and pleasing, who subdued hostile foes. In this way, throughout the great earth under his sway, from the shores of the ocean all the way to the palace, no one used weapons and everyone practiced the true Dharma, and so he made it happy and prosperous.

Son of noble family, the great king of the warrior caste, anointed on the crown of his head into kingship, thus possesses this tenth wheel. By the power of that wheel, all the sentient beings who dwell anywhere from the four great continents to the eight intermediate directions and the eighty-four thousand minor continents abide in the ten courses of virtuous action, his own person is well protected, and his life becomes long. And when he passes on, he is born as a god in the higher realms and comes to possess excellent enjoyments.

Son of noble family, in just the same way, the Tathāgata too, long ago when he was a bodhisattva, knew that his own body and those of others suffered from immeasurable kinds of the disease of the afflictions. So he washed his body with the scented water of meditative absorption, bathed in the water of the Dharma of truth, of great loving-kindness, and of great compassion, and put on the garment of conscience and modesty. All the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the ten directions took him into their care with meditative concentration, meditative attainment, diligence, skillful means, wisdom, loving-kindness, and compassion, and all of them, speaking in unison, said this: "This great being has become a vessel adorned with the great accumulations of merit and wisdom, and so he has become a vessel for the three liberations, the four fearlessnesses, the ten powers of a tathāgata, and the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha. He is the ground for attaining unsurpassed omniscient wisdom. He has perfected great loving-kindness and great compassion. He delights in working for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings. He has become a merchant captain in search of the jewel of buddhahood. He frees the sentient beings who suffer in cyclic existence. He bestows on sentient beings the great bliss of nirvāṇa. We, all the buddhas, the blessed ones, by our word of truth grant him everything whatsoever that he wishes, so that he will fully awaken into perfect and complete awakening, attain the unsurpassed Dharma, and become a great king of Dharma."

At that time, relying on the accumulations of merit and wisdom, I, with exceedingly steadfast diligence, understood the four noble truths exactly as they really are, and fully awakened into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Son of noble family, just as a wheel-turning monarch rules and holds sway over the four continents, so the Tathāgata too has gained mastery over the four meditative concentrations, the four formless absorptions, the four abodes of Brahmā, the four kinds of discriminating knowledge, the four noble truths, the four fearlessnesses, the ten powers of a tathāgata, the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha, and the wisdom that knows all aspects.

Just as a wheel-turning monarch possesses the seven precious things, so the Tathāgata too possesses the seven branches of awakening.

Just as a wheel-turning monarch has a thousand sons, all heroic and bold, handsome and pleasing, who subdue all enemy foes, so the Tathāgata too has a great assembly of śrāvakas, from the first, the noble Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya, to the last, the venerable Subhadra, and others besides, who arose from the heart of the Buddha, who were born from the speech of the Blessed One, who were born from the Dharma he taught, and who have attained the qualities of a buddha. Because they have completely exhausted the defilements, they are called heroic and of great prowess. Because they possess the four abodes of Brahmā, they are called handsome and pleasing. Because they have subdued the divine māras and the non-Buddhists, they are called the subduers of hostile foes.

Just as a wheel-turning monarch tames and guides the eighty-four thousand minor continents, so the Tathāgata too has gained mastery to hold sway by his word over a hundred koṭis of Jambudvīpas, a hundred koṭis of western Godānīyas, a hundred koṭis of eastern Pūrvavidehas, a hundred koṭis of northern Uttarakurus, a hundred koṭis of great oceans, a hundred koṭis of Sumerus, a hundred koṭis of the realms of the Four Great Kings, a hundred koṭis of spheres of neither perception nor non-perception, and a hundred koṭis of great encircling mountain ranges, these having become a single, vast buddha field.

Son of noble family, I thus possess this tenth wheel of a buddha. By the power of that wheel I know, exactly as it really is, that all the defilements of my own body and of others are completely exhausted, and for the sake of the benefit and happiness of immeasurable sentient beings I establish them in the happiness of fearlessness. Being fearless, I dwell in the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, tame all the non-Buddhists who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras, and roar the lion's roar amid a great assembly.

Son of noble family, I thus possess these ten wheels of a buddha. Through the force of my former aspirations I dwell here in this buddha field of the five degenerations, where all sentient beings have lost the pure white qualities, are bereft of the seven noble riches, and have grown far apart from the wise; where they are entangled on every side and overpowered by the net of eternalism and nihilism; where they take pleasure and delight in the chariot that goes to the lower realms; where they have no fear of suffering in the life to come; where they put their faith in dwelling in the darkness of ignorance that always covers everything; where they carry the ten courses of non-virtuous action to completion, commit the five deeds of immediate retribution, denigrate the true Dharma and disparage the noble ones, cast all virtuous qualities far away, and bring non-virtuous qualities to completion. Dwelling here, because I possess these ten kinds of buddha wheel, I abide happily and without fear. Being fearless, I dwell in the place of the great sage, turn the wheel of a buddha, and tame all the non-Buddhists who speak falsely, together with the gods and māras. Having destroyed all the vajra-hard afflictions in the dispositions of all sentient beings, I have established all sentient beings who have become fit vessels, each according to whatever will tame them, so that they will not turn back from the three vehicles.

Then, from within that assembly, all the bodhisattva great beings who were there to be trained, the whole saṅgha of śrāvakas, and all the retinues of gods and nāgas, up to the kaṭapūtanas, humans and non-humans and the rest, the whole great assembly, rejoiced exceedingly and said with one voice, "Excellent!" They sent down a great rain of incense, a great rain of flowers, a great rain of jewels, and a great rain of garments, and this great earth, a hundred thousand of them, quaked violently.

As these ten kinds of buddha wheel were being taught in this way, in that assembly some eighty-four koṭis of niyutas of bodhisattva great beings attained acceptance of the unborn nature of phenomena. Furthermore, an immeasurable number of bodhisattva great beings attained dhāraṇīs and meditative absorptions. Furthermore, an immeasurable, countless number of sentient beings generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening and became irreversible. Furthermore, an immeasurable, countless number of sentient beings attained the fruits.

This concludes the second chapter, the chapter on the ten wheels, from the Great Collection text called The Ten Wheels of Kṣitigarbha, the bodhisattva great being.

The third fascicle.

Then, in that assembly, a great Brahmā called Devagarbha, who had fully developed the roots of virtue, had attained the ten grounds, and possessed the qualities of a bodhisattva great being, rose from his seat, joined his palms, bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One, and spoke these verses:

To you who hold the treasury of qualities, ocean of wisdom,
I put my doubts and make my plea.
Ocean of wisdom, grant me leave out of compassion,
And clear away the doubts that trouble my thinking.
As though we were tormented by a burning thirst,
The supreme Dharma's taste, the essence of all qualities,
The taste of the Dharma of the ultimate, most excellent of all,
All of us gathered in this assembly long to hear.

The Blessed One said to the great Brahmā Devagarbha, "Ask the Tathāgata whatever you wish, and by answering each question you put, I shall satisfy your mind."

The great Brahmā replied, "Blessed One, I shall do so," and he asked in verse:

Those who have wisdom and have well cultivated absorption,
Who dwell at ease in selflessness:
Will they come to abide in the ultimate meaning,
Or will they remain wheeling round in cyclic existence?
Those who, day and night, in that supreme and sublime Dharma
Recite and train themselves and strive:
Will they cross over the lake of the afflictions,
Or will they fall into the lower realms?
Those who strive with great effort in making merit:
Will they surely turn toward nirvāṇa,
Or will they remain wheeling round in cyclic existence,
Or will they fall into the lower realms?
Those who have wisdom, empowered into kingship,
Who are endowed with the ten kinds of wheel:
Will they sink down within cyclic existence,
Or will they attain the fruit of buddhahood?
Those whose minds are seized by the afflictions,
Who are led astray by many afflictions:
How are they to cleanse their minds till they are wholly pure?
How are they to do the work of meditation and recitation?

Then the Blessed One said this to the great Brahmā Devagarbha: "Excellent! It is excellent that you have the eloquence to ask so well about these matters. You devote yourself earnestly to these teachings. You have perfected every kind of conduct. In the presence of past buddhas, blessed ones, as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges, you thoroughly purified your three doors. You have spread the true Dharma. You preserve the lineage of the Three Jewels. And now, for the sake of the benefit and happiness of immeasurable sentient beings, it is excellent that you have asked the Tathāgata about these profound matters.

"Son of noble family, it is like this. There is a teaching called the Great Instruction in Knowledge, on what to abide in and what is not to be practiced. All the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past also taught this teaching, the Great Instruction in Knowledge, on what to abide in and what is not to be practiced, in order to bring all sentient beings to maturity, to make them weary of cyclic existence, to cut off karma and the afflictions completely, and to bring the three vehicles swiftly to perfection.

All the buddhas, the blessed ones, who now dwell in the ten directions also teach abiding by this teaching, the Great Instruction in Knowledge, on what to abide in and what is not to be practiced, in order to bring all sentient beings to maturity, to make them weary of cyclic existence, to cut off karma and the afflictions completely, and to bring the three vehicles swiftly to perfection. All the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the future will also teach these ways of abiding by this teaching, the Great Instruction in Knowledge, on what to abide in and what is not to be practiced, in order to bring all sentient beings to maturity, to make them weary of cyclic existence, to cut off karma and the afflictions completely, and to bring the three vehicles swiftly to perfection. You heard in full, from the tathāgatas of the past, this way of abiding by the teaching of the Great Instruction in Knowledge, on what to abide in and what is not to be practiced, and I too shall teach this way of abiding by the teaching of the Great Instruction in Knowledge, on what to abide in and what is not to be practiced, in order to bring all sentient beings to maturity, to make them weary of cyclic existence, to cut off karma and the afflictions completely, and to bring the three vehicles swiftly to perfection. Therefore, holy being, listen well and keep it in mind, and I shall teach it."

Saying, "Blessed One, I shall do so," the great Brahmā Devagarbha listened to the Blessed One.

The Blessed One said to the great Brahmā Devagarbha, "There are ten kinds of things in which one should not abide and which one should not practice. If yogins follow even a little of this bad conduct, they will not achieve even the roots of virtue of the desire realm; and even if they had achieved them before, these will at once be lost again and decline. What need, then, to speak of their attaining the absorptions of the form and formless realms, up to the three vehicles and the one vehicle?

"What are the ten? They are these. Some in the world wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but, being busy with the necessities of life, they are afflicted through the circumstance of having to seek them. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but their moral discipline is broken and they commit many wrongdoings. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but they hold distorted views and are attached to the good and bad omens of the world, and their bodies and minds are untamed. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but because their minds are restless and excited they do not incline toward the noble ones, and their senses are unruly. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but they delight in slander and so cause dissension. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but with harsh words they revile and disparage the noble ones. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but they engage in idle talk and tell lies. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but out of desire and jealousy they are displeased at the profit, honor, and gain of others. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but being full of aversion they give rise to malice toward sentient beings. Some wish to cultivate meditative absorption, but having wrong views they hold that there is no cause and no result.

"Great Brahmā, understand it thus: these are called the ten kinds of things in which one should not abide and which one should not practice. If those who cultivate meditative absorption follow even a little of this bad conduct, they will not achieve even the virtue of the desire realm; and even if they had achieved it before, it will at once be lost and decline. What need, then, to speak of their attaining the absorptions of the form and formless realms, and the three vehicles and the one?

"Great Brahmā, furthermore, there are ten kinds of things in which one should not abide and which one should not practice. If those who cultivate meditative absorption have any of this bad conduct, they will not achieve meditative absorption; and even if they had achieved it before, it will at once be lost and decline. What are the ten? They are these: delight in and attachment to tasks and activities; delight in and attachment to idle talk; delight in and attachment to sleep; delight in and attachment to seeking material things; delight in and attachment to beautiful forms; delight in and attachment to pleasant sounds; delight in and attachment to fragrant scents; delight in and attachment to delicious tastes; delight in and attachment to soft textures; and delight in and attachment to conceptual thought and analysis.

"Great Brahmā, understand it thus: if those who cultivate meditative absorption have any of this bad conduct, these ten kinds of things in which one should not abide and which one should not practice, they will not achieve the meditative absorptions; and even if they had achieved them before, these will at once be lost and decline.

"If meditative absorption has not been achieved, then whatever other accumulations of virtue one gathers will not become meaningful. For in order to secure one's own enjoyments and the gifts given out of faith, harmful states of mind and of mental factors will arise. Because of that, one will even break the laws of the king and the great ministers. One will insult and abuse some people, flog some with whips, and cut off or have cut off the limbs and extremities of others. By that cause and that condition one will be struck by severe illness and suffer for a long time, one's life will be short, and after death and transmigration one will be born in one or another of the three lower realms, falling as far as the hell of Unceasing Torment. Like Kudaka, Arādaka, Deśukupālaki, Devadatta, and others, one will fall away from meditative concentration, fall as far as the hell of Unceasing Torment, and experience every kind of great, unbearable suffering in immeasurable forms."

At that time the Blessed One said this to the venerable Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya: "I permit you, and the monks who dwell in remote places and cultivate meditative absorption, to stay in the best lodgings, to make use of the best bedding and the best food, and not to perform any of the work of the Saṅgha. Why is that? If those who cultivate meditative absorption become busy with the necessities of life, harmful states of mind and of mental factors will arise, they will fail to achieve any meditative absorption, and they will fall as far as the hell of Unceasing Torment and experience great, unbearable suffering in immeasurable forms.

"If those who cultivate meditative absorption are provided with the necessities of life, they will achieve the many meditative absorptions they had not achieved, and those they achieved before will not decline. By virtue of this, no non-virtuous states at all will arise in them; and in the same way, following the text at length, up to non-virtuous conceptual thought and analysis, none will arise. Then they will attain everything from birth as gods in the higher realms up to nirvāṇa.

"If those who cultivate meditative absorption have not achieved the many meditative absorptions, they should not sleep in the first and last watches of the night, but train and meditate with diligence. They should give up busyness entirely and be of few wants and content, and not be attached to anything. They give up entirely all desire, aversion, anger, concealment, agitation, harmfulness, pride, conceit, jealousy, avarice, slander, harsh speech, lies, worldly quarrels, and heedlessness. Whoever strives in this way may accept the praise, acclaim, homage, honor, service, attendance, and so on, and even hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of gifts, from Śakra, Brahmā, the four guardians of the world, wheel-turning monarchs and the like; what need, then, to speak of accepting the offerings of the warrior caste, brahmins, the merchant caste and the rest? Even those who have not attained meditative absorption are entitled to take as their own the praise, acclaim, homage, honor, service, attendance, gifts, and offerings of these; what need, then, to speak of those who have attained meditative absorption?"

Then the Blessed One spoke in verse:

By meditating on absorption, doubts are cut away;
Nothing else apart from this is able to do so.
Therefore the cultivation of absorption is supreme,
And the wise should honor it.

Then the great Brahmā Devagarbha said this: "Venerable Blessed One, if those who have gone forth in the teaching of the Buddha are flogged or beaten with whips, staffs and the like by those of the warrior caste, by great ministers, or by officials, or if they are put in prison, reviled, mocked, have their limbs cut off, or are deprived of life and killed, is that proper, or is it not proper?"

The Blessed One said this to the great Brahmā Devagarbha: "Son of noble family, when any sentient being has gone forth in my teaching, shaved off hair and beard, and put on the saffron robe, then whether he keeps his moral discipline, or has broken his moral discipline, or even has no moral discipline at all, gods, humans, asuras and all the rest do not allow him even to be subjected to the rules that apply to householders. What need, then, to speak of subjecting him to what is contrary to the Dharma: flogging and beating his body with whips, staffs and the like, putting him in prison, insulting him, abusing him, cutting off his limbs, or depriving him of his life faculty?

"Why is that? Leave aside those who keep their moral discipline and are very learned. Even an evil monk who has gone forth in my teaching and broken his moral discipline, who by practicing non-virtuous things has become rotten within, like a worm-eaten husk filled with filth; who claims to be a renunciant when he is no renunciant, and claims to live the pure life when he does not live the pure life; who is defeated by afflictions of every kind, ruined, and fallen headlong: even such an evil monk of broken discipline becomes a teacher and guide for all the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, humans, non-humans and the rest, and becomes a treasury of immeasurable precious qualities.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, even when those who have gone forth in my teaching have broken their moral discipline, merely by seeing their form sentient beings give rise to ten kinds of superior intention, and so they will gain ten immeasurable heaps of precious qualities. What are the ten? They are these.

"Though those who have gone forth in my teaching may have broken their moral discipline, when sentient beings see them, they recollect the Buddha and give rise to a superior intention of faith and reverence, accompanied by respect. By that cause and that condition they will never turn toward non-Buddhist teachers, their treatises, or their followers, and so on, up to entering the great city of complete nirvāṇa, free from fear.

"Some, on seeing them, give rise to a superior intention of recollecting noble moral discipline. By that cause and that condition they will entirely give up taking life, entirely give up taking what is not given, entirely give up sexual misconduct, entirely give up lying, and entirely give up drinking beer and other intoxicating liquors that are a basis for heedlessness, and so on, up to entering the great city of complete nirvāṇa, free from fear.

"Some, on seeing them, give rise to a superior intention of recollecting generosity. By that cause and that condition they will come to have great wealth and to make offerings to and serve the genuine and those of genuine conduct, and so on, up to entering the great city of complete nirvāṇa, free from fear.

"Some, on seeing them, give rise to a superior intention of recollecting the patience of a gentle nature. By that cause and that condition they will entirely give up slander, harsh speech, idle talk, and aversion, and so on, up to entering the great city of complete nirvāṇa, free from fear.

"Some, on seeing them, give rise to a superior intention of recollecting going forth and devoting oneself to diligence. By that cause and that condition they will entirely give up the household, take up the way of homelessness, and devote themselves with diligence of the utmost effort to the supreme conduct, and so on, up to entering the great city of complete nirvāṇa, without fear.

"Some, on seeing them, give rise to a superior intention of recollecting giving up the agitated mind and settling evenly in meditative concentration. By that cause and that condition they will delight in mountains, groves, and remote hermitages and, day and night, apply diligence to cultivating meditative absorption, and so on, up to entering the great city of complete nirvāṇa, without fear.

"Some, on seeing them, give rise to a superior intention of recollecting wisdom.

By that cause and that condition they will delight and rejoice in hearing, reading, and reciting the sublime Dharma, and so on, up to entering the great city of complete nirvāṇa.

"Some, on seeing them, give rise to a superior intention of recollecting the roots of virtue they generated before for the sake of renunciation, and so on, up to speaking with a smile, conversing with delight, and bowing at their feet. By that cause and that condition they will be born into great and high families and into noble families, and when countless sentient beings see them they will rejoice, and so on, up to entering, free from fear, the great city of complete nirvāṇa.

"Son of good family, even when those who have gone forth in my teaching have broken their moral discipline, merely by seeing their form and their robes sentient beings give rise to these ten kinds of superior intention, and they will obtain immeasurable heaps of precious qualities. Therefore it is not right for kings, great ministers, governors, and the like to beat and strike them with whips, staves, and the like, to throw them into prison, to revile and abuse them, to cut off their limbs, to deprive them of their life faculty, and so forth.

"Moreover, great Brahmā, when those who have gone for refuge to me and gone forth break their moral discipline and practice unwholesome dharmas, I have called such a one a monk who is like a worm-ridden thing full of rot and filth; one who, not being a śramaṇa, claims to be a śramaṇa; who, not living the pure life, claims to live the pure life; defeated by afflictions of many kinds; rotten; overturned, as it were. Yet although such a monk has broken his moral discipline and practices what is unwholesome, he acts as a teacher and a guiding spiritual friend for all gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, humans, nonhumans, and the rest, and he becomes a treasury of immeasurable qualities. Thus, although that monk is not a vessel for the Dharma, he has shaved his hair and beard and put on the saffron robes, and his appearance and deportment accord with those of the noble ones. Merely by seeing him, countless sentient beings will increase and generate roots of virtue of many different kinds. Furthermore, countless sentient beings will be born as gods in the higher realms and will be set upon the true path of passing into nirvāṇa. Therefore, whether those who have gone for refuge to me and gone forth keep their moral discipline, have broken their moral discipline, or have no moral discipline at all, if a universal monarch, another king, a minister, or the like is not permitted even to make them return to the state of a householder, what need is there to speak of other unlawful acts, such as beating them with whips, staves, and the like, striking them, throwing them into prison, reviling and abusing them, cutting and chopping off their limbs and extremities, depriving them of their life faculty, and so forth?

"Great Brahmā, although I have declared that such a monk who has broken his moral discipline and commits evil is like a corpse within the discipline I have taught, the remaining power of his going forth, his moral discipline, and his qualities is still present. For example, when a bull or a musk deer dies and is parted from consciousness, it is a corpse among the animals; yet because the bull has bezoar and the musk deer has musk, they become of great value for the benefit and happiness of countless, immeasurable sentient beings. In just the same way, although that monk who has broken his moral discipline is called a corpse within the discipline I have taught, through the remaining power of his going forth, his moral discipline, and his qualities he too becomes of great value for the benefit and happiness of countless, immeasurable sentient beings.

"Great Brahmā, it is like this. Merchants set out upon the great ocean and kill countless creatures of a single species and take out their eyes. If the eyeballs are combined with the fruit called Steadfast, they become a precious eye medicine. When this precious eye medicine is applied to the eyes of beings who are blind and eyeless, who came out of the womb blind from birth, they are freed from every disease of the eye, and their eyes become keen and clear. In the same way, although the monk who has broken his moral discipline is called a corpse within the discipline I have taught, because he has the deportment and the appearance of one gone forth, countless sentient beings, merely by seeing him for an instant, will obtain the utterly pure eye of wisdom; what need is there to speak of his being able to teach the sublime Dharma to others?

"Great Brahmā, it is like this. When incense is burned, although the incense itself is consumed and disappears, its fragrance lets others smell it. In the same way, that monk who has broken his moral discipline is, because he has broken it, not a fertile field of merit; burned day and night by what is given in faith, when he passes from this life he falls into the three lower realms. Yet he serves the benefit and happiness of countless, immeasurable sentient beings, causing them to be born as gods in the higher realms and to catch the fragrance of passing into nirvāṇa. Therefore, great Brahmā, I do not permit anyone to beat and strike with whips and staves, throw into prison, revile and abuse, cut off the limbs of, or deprive of the life faculty such a monk who has broken his moral discipline and commits evil, one gone forth whom all householders should honor with reverence and serve.

"Within the pure Saṅgha, such monks are to be excluded from the poṣadha, from entering the rains retreat, and from the ceremony at its close, and they have no right to make use of anything belonging to the Saṅgha of the four directions: its gains, its goods, its food, its drink, its requisites for living, and the like.
