It is only that they are not permitted to come into any of the Vinaya proceedings carried out by the śramaṇas; but I do not permit anyone to beat them, strike them, bind them, deprive them of the life faculty, and so forth."

Then the Blessed One spoke in verse:

"Though the campaka flower has withered,
It far surpasses every other kind of flower.
Though a monk has broken his discipline and does evil,
He is supreme above all the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas)."

"Furthermore, great Brahmā, there are five kinds of action, great misdeeds of immediate retribution.

What are the five? Deliberately killing one's father, deliberately killing one's mother, deliberately killing an arhat, causing a schism in the Saṅgha of śrāvakas through wrong view, and with malicious intent drawing blood from the body of a buddha. These five kinds are called the actions that are great misdeeds of immediate retribution. If any person has committed even one of these five, it is not permissible for him to go forth or to receive full ordination. If someone does give him the going forth or full ordination, his preceptor is at fault and is to be expelled from the Saṅgha. Even so, because such a person, once gone forth, has the deportment and appearance, I do not permit anyone to beat and strike him with whips and staves, throw him into prison, revile and abuse him, cut off his limbs, or deprive him of his life faculty.

"Furthermore, there are four kinds of action that are close to the great misdeeds of immediate retribution. What are the four? To kill a pratyekabuddha with an unwholesome mind: among the acts of taking life this is an action of great misdeed, and it is a root offense. To engage in unchaste conduct with a nun who is an arhat: among acts of sexual misconduct this is an action of great misdeed, and so it is a root offense. To make use of the property of the Three Jewels given in faith: among acts of taking what is not given this is an action of great misdeed, and so it is a root offense. To cause a schism in a harmonious Saṅgha through evil views: among acts of false speech this is an action of great misdeed, and so it is a root offense. If any person has committed even one of these four kinds of action close to the great misdeeds of immediate retribution, I do not permit him to go forth or to receive full ordination. If someone does give him the going forth or full ordination, his preceptor is at fault and is to be expelled from my teaching. Even so, if such a person has gone forth or been fully ordained, because he possesses the deportment and appearance, I do not permit anyone to beat and strike him with whips and staves, to revile him, to abuse him, to throw him into prison, to cut off his limbs, and so on, up to depriving him of his life faculty.

"Thus there are some that are root evils but not of immediate retribution. There are some that are of immediate retribution but not root evils. There are some that are both root evils and evils of immediate retribution. And there are some that are neither root evils nor of immediate retribution.

"Now, what is it that is both a root evil and of immediate retribution? It is this: when one who has gone forth in my teaching and received full ordination deliberately kills a person who has reached the far shore of seeing the truths. This is both a root evil and of immediate retribution, and so he must quickly be expelled from the Dharma of the discipline I have taught.

"What is it that is a root evil but not of immediate retribution? It is this: when one who has gone forth in my teaching and received full ordination deliberately takes the life of another person, or, at the least, kills a being dwelling in the womb by giving poison. These are root evils but not of immediate retribution. I do not permit such a person to stay with the Saṅgha even for a night, or to make use of the goods and the property of the Saṅgha of the four directions.

"What is it that is of immediate retribution but not a root evil? It is this: when a person who has gone for refuge to the Three Jewels, or who holds the five precepts, or who holds the ten precepts, has committed even one of the five acts of immediate retribution, this is called an evil of immediate retribution but not a root evil. It is not permissible to give such a person the going forth or full ordination. If someone does give him the going forth or full ordination, his preceptor is tainted with evil and is to be expelled from my teaching.

"What is it that is neither a root evil nor of immediate retribution? It is this: when a person who has gone for refuge to the Three Jewels, or who has taken the five precepts, gives rise to a mind that doubts the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha, goes for refuge to non-Buddhists and takes them as his teacher and master, or regards all sorts of portents as omens and follows worldly gods. And when any person disparages and misrepresents the sublime Dharma taught by the Tathāgata, the sublime Dharma connected with the Śrāvaka Vehicle, the sublime Dharma connected with the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, and the sublime Dharma connected with the Great Vehicle; when he obstructs and hinders it; when, himself having no trust and no faith, he turns others away from it and hinders them; and when he cuts off and obstructs others' reading, reciting, or copying into a book even a single verse of it: this is neither a root evil nor of immediate retribution, but is called an action of great misdeed close to immediate retribution.

"Until such a person has confessed and disclosed these great evil actions of misdeed, it is not permissible for him to go forth or to receive full ordination. If anyone gives him the going forth or full ordination, that preceptor is tainted with evil and is to be expelled from my teaching. If such a person, having gone forth or been fully ordained, does not perform the act of confessing and disclosing that evil misdeed, he must quickly be expelled from the discipline I have taught. Why is that? Because these two kinds of person destroy the eye of the sublime Dharma, extinguish the lamp of the sublime Dharma, and utterly cut off the lineage of the Three Jewels. They lead gods, humans, and others to undergo suffering that is pointless and without benefit, and they make them fall into the lower realms.

These two kinds of person themselves disparage the sublime Dharma and revile the noble ones, and they also lead others to disparage the sublime Dharma and revile the noble ones. Immediately upon passing from this life they fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment and experience suffering for an eon, and they are beyond remedy.

"Moreover, great Brahmā, there are evils by prohibition that exclude one from communion and must not be done; there are evils by nature that exclude one from communion and must not be done; and among the evils by nature there are those that become root evils, which exclude one from communion and must not be done. Now, what are the root evils, the things that exclude one from communion and must not be done? They are these: when a monk commits a root evil by engaging in unchaste conduct; commits a root evil by deliberately taking the life of a human being; commits a root evil by stealing what is not the property of the Three Jewels; or commits a root evil by telling a great lie. If a monk has committed any of these four kinds of root evil, he is not included in the formal acts performed by the monks, and he has no right to make use of any goods or of the property of the Saṅgha of the four directions. Yet I do not permit anyone to do anything to him such as beating and striking him with staves and whips, throwing him into prison, abusing and reviling him, cutting off his limbs, or depriving him of his life faculty. Among the evils by nature these are the grave root evils; they are called the things that exclude one from communion and must not be done.

"Why are they called root evils? If any person commits these four kinds of deed, immediately upon passing from this life he will fall into the lower realms. Because they are the root of going to the lower realms, they are called root evils.

"Why, then, are the acts of immediate retribution, the acts close to immediate retribution, and the root evils spoken of as evil actions of great misdeed that exclude one from communion and must not be done? Son of good family, it is like this. If a lump of iron or a ball of lead is thrown up into the sky above, it does not stay there even for a moment but at once falls back to the ground. So it is with the five acts of immediate retribution, the acts close to immediate retribution, the four root evils, disparaging the sublime Dharma, doubting the Three Jewels, and the two kinds of evil person. If any person commits any of these eleven kinds of evil, immediately upon passing from this life he is without obstruction and without doubt born in the great hell of Unceasing Torment and experiences great suffering. Therefore they are called evil actions of great misdeed that exclude one from communion and must not be done. A person who has committed these evil actions of great misdeed that exclude one from communion and must not be done is certainly unable to exhaust the afflictions in this very body. He does not even accomplish meditative absorption, let alone enter and attain the unchanging certainty of the right path. Immediately upon passing from this life he will without doubt be born in hell and experience great suffering.

"Moreover, great Brahmā, any son or daughter of good family who with pure intention has faith in my teaching and has entered the Śrāvaka Vehicle, or entered the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, or entered the Great Vehicle, and who out of faith in what I have taught has gone forth from home into homelessness and received full ordination, should hold the many points of training in great honor and respect, and should undertake great diligence, striving intensely to guard against the four root evils. Gods, humans, nonhumans, and the rest will always follow and protect such a person, and the offerings of gods, humans, and others made to him will not go to waste. In accordance with whichever of the three vehicles he delights and rejoices in, he will swiftly reach its final state. Therefore those who wish for true nirvāṇa should not let these four kinds of principle decline, for it would be easier to lose body and life.

"Why is that? Because ordinary beings attain the bliss of nirvāṇa through these three kinds of cause. What are the three? The cause that relies on the Tathāgata, the cause that relies on what the noble ones teach, and the cause that relies on my close disciples: these are the three. If ordinary beings rely on these three kinds of cause and apply themselves earnestly with diligence, they will attain the bliss of nirvāṇa.

"If any person lets these four kinds of principle decline, I am not his teacher, and he is not my śrāvaka. If any person lets these four kinds of principle decline, he goes against the prātimokṣa I have taught, which is very profound and vast, endowed with impermanence, suffering, emptiness, and selflessness, and taught for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings. If he goes against the prātimokṣa I have taught, he becomes blind and confused with regard to all meditative concentrations and meditative absorptions and is unable to incline toward them or enter them. Entangled and overpowered by many afflictions, he is not a vessel for the Dharma of the three vehicles, and so he falls into the lower realms and experiences great suffering.

"If a son or daughter of good family has not let the four roots of the prātimokṣa I have taught decline but keeps them perfectly pure, then I am his teacher and he is my śrāvaka. He follows my words, dwells happily in my Dharma, and has brought every task to completion. Because such a person is firmly established in the aggregate of moral discipline, he is well established in all wholesome dharmas. He is established in the perfection of the Śrāvaka Vehicle. He is established in the perfection of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle. He is established in the perfection of the Great Vehicle.

"Why is that? Because if one guards against these four roots of evil by nature, that is established as the supreme and sublime cause of all wholesome dharmas, both those with defilements and those without defilements. Therefore guarding these four kinds of principle is called the root of all wholesome dharmas. It is like this: in dependence on the great earth all herbs and trees, crops, fruit trees, forests, and the like grow. In the same way, in dependence on guarding these four roots of moral discipline, all wholesome dharmas increase and grow.

"It is like this: in dependence on the great earth all mountains, the Cakravāla range, the Great Cakravāla range, Meru the king of mountains, and the rest abide securely. In the same way, in dependence on guarding the four roots of moral discipline, the Śrāvaka Vehicle, the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, and the unsurpassed Great Vehicle will abide securely. It is like this: in dependence on the great earth, all the finest flavors in the world are found by seeking them. In the same way, in dependence on guarding the four roots of moral discipline, one will attain mindfulness, meditative absorption, dhāraṇī, gentle patience, the whole noble path, and so on, up to unsurpassed, perfectly complete awakening. It is like this: the great earth bears everything equally, the clean and the unclean. In the same way, if sons and daughters of good family who carefully guard the four roots of moral discipline settle their minds evenly toward those who have become vessels for the Dharma and those who have not, and do not despise them, do not revile them, do not praise themselves, are not conceited, and do not find fault, they become a ground from which all wholesome dharmas grow. It is like this: all sentient beings make use of this great earth and live happily on it. In the same way, sons and daughters of good family who carefully guard the four roots of moral discipline, with great delight in the true Dharma taught by the Tathāgata and with a supreme and sublime pure intention, gather all sentient beings equally by means of the four ways of gathering, without any perception of distinction, and so they cause all sentient beings to enjoy the delight of the Dharma and to live happily."

Then the venerable Upāli, having heard what the Blessed One had said, rose from his seat, arranged his robe, touched his head to the Blessed One's feet, placed his upper robe over one shoulder, set his right knee on the ground, bowed with palms joined, and said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, you have said that sons and daughters of good family who carefully guard the four roots of moral discipline settle their minds evenly toward all, those who have become vessels for the Dharma and those who have not; that they do not despise them, do not revile them, do not praise themselves, are not conceited, and do not abruptly raise charges of fault. If that is so, then at a time when you are no longer present, by what means should the Saṅgha of monks reprove and remove monks who have broken their moral discipline and engage in what is unwholesome, who, not being śramaṇas, claim to be śramaṇas, and who, not living the pure life, claim to live the pure life?"

The Blessed One said to the venerable Upāli, "I give no occasion at all for non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) and householders to be permitted to raise charges against a monk's faults. If even the Saṅgha of monks is not permitted to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline in a way that is not in accord with the Dharma and without proper investigation, what need is there to speak of removing or expelling him? If they raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline, or expel him, in a way that is not in accord with the Dharma and without proper investigation, they incur the evil of a great misdeed.

"Upāli, understand it like this. Whoever raises charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline in these ten ways that are not in accord with the Dharma and without proper investigation will incur a great evil of misdeed, so the wise do not act like that. What are the ten? Without the Saṅgha's consent, to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline in the presence of the king, without proper investigation; without the Saṅgha's consent, to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before wandering ascetics; without the Saṅgha's consent, to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before ministers; without the Saṅgha's consent, to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before merchants and householders; to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before many women; to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before many men; to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before many who act improperly; to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before many monks and nuns; to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before those who bear him a grudge; and to raise charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline before hostile enemies, without proper investigation. These are called the ten ways in which, if one raises charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline in a way that is not the Dharma and without proper investigation, one will incur a great evil of misdeed. If one is at fault even when one raises charges based on what is true and correct, what need is there to speak of raising charges based on what is untrue? One will incur a great evil of misdeed.

"Furthermore, whoever raises charges against the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline in these ten ways that are not the Dharma will incur the evil of a great misdeed, so the wise do not act like that. What are the ten? For non-Buddhists to raise charges against a monk's faults; for householders without moral discipline to raise charges against a monk's faults; for those who have committed acts of immediate retribution to raise charges against a monk's faults; for those who disparage the sublime Dharma to raise charges against a monk's faults; for those who revile the noble ones to raise charges against a monk's faults; for the foolish and mentally disturbed to raise charges against a monk's faults; for those tormented by illness to raise charges against a monk's faults; for the attendants who serve the Saṅgha of the four directions to raise charges against a monk's faults; for those who guard the orchards to raise charges against a monk's faults; and for monks under penalty to raise charges against a monk's faults. If the faults of a monk who has broken his moral discipline are charged in these ten ways that are not the Dharma, one will incur a great evil of misdeed. If one is at fault even when one raises charges based on what is true and correct, what need is there to speak of raising charges falsely? One will incur a great evil of misdeed.

"Furthermore, Upāli, if monks who have broken their moral discipline are living together with the Saṅgha within the Saṅgha of monks, then one monk among them who acts properly, who has good deportment, and who possesses the five qualities should rise from his seat, arrange his robe, and with reverence bow at the feet of the Saṅgha of monks. Then he should go before the monk who has broken his moral discipline and obtain his leave to raise a charge of fault, saying: 'Venerable one, please attend. Today I shall raise a charge regarding your fault. I raise it truthfully, not falsely; at the proper time, not letting the time pass; speaking with a smile, in words that are not harsh, with a mind of loving-kindness and without aversion; for your benefit, without harm to your happiness; and so that the Tathāgata's eye of the Dharma and lamp of the Dharma may long remain. Venerable one, I raise a charge regarding your fault in accordance with the Dharma.' If that person permits the charge to be raised, it should be raised in accordance with the Dharma. If he does not permit it, then the monk should again bow at the feet of the elders and of the Saṅgha and with reverence say this: 'The monk named so-and-so has committed such-and-such a fault, and I wish to raise a charge on the basis of the five principles.' Then the elder monks within the Saṅgha should investigate thoroughly, questioning the accuser about what is charged and what fault was committed, and determining whether it is true or false, heavy or light; and relying on the Vinaya and the sūtras they should question and examine him with various means of admonition and drawing out the truth. By the seven methods of settling disputes, it should be settled by whichever method is appropriate in each case. If he has committed a great fault, he is to be punished with a great penalty. If he has committed a middling fault, he is to be punished with a middling penalty. If he has committed a small fault, he is to be punished with a small penalty, and he should be made to confess the fault with shame and embarrassment."

Then Upāli again said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, suppose monks who have committed evil that is a genuine fault are arrogant in the power of householders, or arrogant in the power of wealth, or arrogant in the power of great learning, the power of eloquence, or the power of disciples, and, stiff-necked with such powers, do not listen to the word of the Saṅgha; and suppose that, although the elder monks who hold the sūtras, the Vinaya, and the mātṛkā teach and correct them in accordance with the Dharma, they refuse to listen. How should such monks be punished?"

The Blessed One said, "The elder monks who hold the three baskets should ask the consent of the Saṅgha and send a messenger to the king and the great ministers to seek their power, and only then punish them in accordance with the Dharma."

Upāli again said, "Blessed One, suppose that monk who commits evil, by the power of riches, the power of great learning, the power of eloquence, and all sorts of means, so pleases the king and ministers that they all act against the Dharma and become allies of the monk who has broken his moral discipline; that they let those monks of merely bad conduct commit evil as they please, and will not even allow them to be punished in accordance with the Dharma. At such a time, what should the Saṅgha do about him?"

The Blessed One said to Upāli, "If that monk, though dwelling in an offense that excludes one from communion and must not be done, lives within the Saṅgha and his great evil is not directly evident, then for the time being the Saṅgha should leave him be. If that monk lives within the Saṅgha while his great evil that excludes one from communion and must not be done is directly evident, then the Saṅgha should ask consent, expel him in accordance with the Dharma, and cast him out of the Buddha's Dharma.

"Upāli, it is like this. Within a field of barley there grows a weed harmful to the barley. Although in its sprout, its stalk, and its joints it resembles barley, it spoils the good barley. While that weed has not yet put out its ear, the owner of the barley leaves it for a time. As soon as it puts out its ear, the owner of the barley thinks, 'It is spoiling the good barley,' pulls it out by the roots, and throws it outside the field. In the same way, that monk who has broken his moral discipline, who is in an offense that excludes one from communion and must not be done, is arrogant in householders and in many kinds of power. He lives within the Saṅgha, and although in deportment and appearance he resembles the Saṅgha, he spoils the pure Saṅgha. So long as the guardian gods have not exposed him and he lives within the Saṅgha with his great evil not directly evident, the Saṅgha should leave him be for the time being. If, urged on by the guardian gods too, his great fault becomes directly evident within the Saṅgha, then the Saṅgha should ask consent and expel him from the Buddha's Dharma. Upāli, it is like this: the great ocean does not let a corpse remain there overnight. In the same way, the disciples of the Saṅgha of my śrāvakas do not associate with a monk who has broken his moral discipline and commits evil, who is like a corpse."

Upāli again said, "Blessed One, suppose that even after the Saṅgha has asked consent and expelled that monk who has broken his moral discipline and commits evil, he so pleases the king and the great ministers by the power of precious wealth, the power of great learning, the power of eloquence, and all sorts of means that they all become allies of that man of bad conduct; and they forcibly reinstate him by their great power, so that the monk who has broken his moral discipline lives together with the Saṅgha. At such a time, what should the Saṅgha do about him?"

The Blessed One said, "At such a time, the monks within the Saṅgha who have scruples, a sense of shame, and moral discipline should, in order to guard their moral discipline, not give rise to aversion toward that monk who has broken his moral discipline, and, without bringing harm upon the king and the great ministers, should take their leave, abandon that place, and go to another place."

The fourth fascicle.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Kṣitigarbha, said this to the Blessed One:

"Venerable Blessed One, in a buddhafield marked by the five degenerations, where no tathāgata dwells, the beings who live there at that time are tormented by afflictions, practice what is unwholesome, and become dull by nature. They are extremely hard to tame: caṇḍāla kṣatriyas (rulers who behave like outcastes), caṇḍāla ministers, caṇḍāla householders, caṇḍāla merchants, caṇḍāla brahmins, and others like them. Their roots of virtue are slight, they have no faith in their minds, and they live by deceit and guile. These fools are conceited about their knowledge, have no fear of the fruit of suffering in the next world, and reject spiritual friends. For the sake of gain and profit they associate with monks who have broken their moral discipline and commit evil, and they become their companions in conduct contrary to the Dharma, so that all of them, without any doubt, enter and descend into the hell of Unceasing Torment. I go to those places where such beings enter and descend into the hell of Unceasing Torment, and through the Dharma of the buddhas, the blessed ones, for the benefit, happiness and relief of those beings, I teach them with skillful means the unsurpassed and sacred Dharma that is like nectar, guide them to it, and set them to practicing it. Thus I turn them back, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla brahmins, from falling into the hell of Unceasing Torment."

The Blessed One said this to the bodhisattva, the great being Kṣitigarbha:

"Son of noble family, in the future, in a later time, there will be beings in this buddhafield who are thoroughly consumed by afflictions, who commit evil, and who become childish, deluded and hard to tame: caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, caṇḍāla ministers, caṇḍāla householders, caṇḍāla merchants, caṇḍāla ascetics, caṇḍāla brahmins, and others. Their roots of virtue are slight, they have no faith in their minds, and they live by deceit and guile. They are deluded and conceited about their knowledge. They reject spiritual friends. There is no truth in the words they speak. They do not follow what spiritual friends teach, but despise them and lord it over them, and they beat them down with harsh words. They doubt the sacred Dharma and hold wrong views. They have no fear of the fruit of suffering in the next world. They always delight in associating with people of bad character. They delight in conduct ranging from killing living beings to holding wrong views. They deceive the world with deceit and guile, and they bring harm upon themselves and others. These, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla brahmins, will destroy my Dharma.

"And there will be those who, though they have gone forth in my teaching, have broken their moral discipline. They are eager to engage in the busy affairs of the world, and are honored with reverence by kṣatriyas, brahmins and others. Greedy for profit and seeking wealth, they speak mere words and do not live by them. They delight in carrying messages back and forth and delivering letters. They engage in trade and the pursuit of profit, study the treatises of the Lokāyata materialists, and delight in the work of plowing fields. They hoard and hide wealth and jewels, and look after houses, wives and children. They subjugate bhūtas with worldly mantras and mudrās, read good and bad omens, and live on the payment they receive for compounding medicines and curing illnesses. They are attached to food, drink, clothing and jeweled ornaments, and delight in doing the work of householders. They have only a semblance of moral discipline while they commit evil, and they behave like dogs at the sound of the conch. Though they are not ascetics, they claim to be ascetics, and though they do not practice pure conduct, they claim to practice pure conduct. The caṇḍāla kṣatriyas through the caṇḍāla brahmins will attend on them, make offerings to them with reverence, and listen to their words. And those who have broken their moral discipline will in turn delight in associating with the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas through the caṇḍāla brahmins, will show them honor and service, and will listen to their words.

"But if some person who has gone forth in my teaching possesses moral discipline and good qualities, whether he is one who applies himself to the stages of training and of no more training or one who has reached the culmination of the fruit, then the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas through the caṇḍāla brahmins will be jealous of him. They will not attend on him, revere him, serve him or make offerings to him, nor will they even listen to his words.

"Son of noble family, it is like this. A certain man goes to an island of jewels, and there he casts aside the jewels indranīla and mahānīla, gold, silver, pearls, rubies, beryl and many other kinds of priceless jewels, and takes glass beads instead. In just the same way, in the future, in this buddhafield, the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas through the caṇḍāla brahmins will do likewise. Having reached the jewel island of the sacred Dharma that I have taught, they will cast aside the assembly of noble ones: persons in training and beyond training who possess moral discipline and good qualities, who delight in the ultimate, and who have scruples and a sense of shame, as well as ordinary beings endowed with virtuous qualities who strive diligently in the six perfections. Instead they will take as fields of merit the evil monks who have broken their moral discipline, who commit many evil deeds, who have no shame and no scruples, whose speech is coarse and harsh, whose bodies and minds are swollen with pride, who are devoid of pure and wholesome qualities, and who have no loving-kindness and no compassion. They will honor, serve and make offerings to them, and listen to their words. Thus these evil ones, teachers and disciples alike, will without any doubt fall into the hell of Unceasing Torment."

"Son of noble family, there are ten kinds of wheels of evil. In the future, in this buddhafield, if caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, caṇḍāla vassal lords, caṇḍāla householders, caṇḍāla merchants, caṇḍāla ascetics and caṇḍāla brahmins possess some of these ten kinds of wheels of evil, or indeed all of them, then all the roots of virtue they accumulated in the past will be burned and become like ashes. Before long their limbs will be cut to pieces, for many days and nights their tongues will be stiff so that they cannot speak, and they will experience great and unending suffering. And when they have died, they will without any doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment.

"What are the ten? They are as follows. The kṣatriyas, the ministers and others tolerate the wicked views of monks who have thus broken their moral discipline and commit evil. But when, on the other hand, they see pure monks who dwell in the remote forest, they say this about them: 'O sirs, these monks are fools and deluded. They are hypocrites who deceive the world. Out of desire for food, drink, clothing, profit, gain and fame, they praise themselves, disparage others, and stir up discord out of jealousy. They are hungry for profit and fame and never satisfied, so they deserve to be driven out, and you should not listen to their words. These monks speak nothing but lies and are devoid of the true Dharma, so they have not attained any fruit. They are not free of desire, nor have they exhausted the defilements. It is only because they want profit, fame and offerings that they live in the remote forest and present themselves as possessing good qualities. Do not make offerings to them, nor attend on them or show them reverence. They live by deceit and guile; they are not a true field of merit, nor do they apply themselves to the path.' In this way they slander them.

"Then all of them, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla brahmins, no longer give rise to faith toward the pure monks who dwell in the remote forest. They no longer regard them as rare, and have no wish to show them reverence. They are not inclined to make offerings to them or attend on them; however much those monks teach, they do not listen to their words, but despise and disparage them.

"To despise and disparage in this way the pure monks who dwell in the remote forest is itself to despise and disparage the eye of the Dharma and the lineage of the Three Jewels. Therefore at that time the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, deities and others who dwell in that land, whose minds are pure toward the Three Jewels and whose faith is unshakable, grow angry with all those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla brahmins. Feelings of aversion arise in them, and they say to one another: 'O sirs, look! These caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, vassal lords, householders, merchants, ascetics and caṇḍāla brahmins despise and lord it over all those who carry on the eye of the Dharma and the lineage of the Three Jewels. They have thereby destroyed their own roots of virtue, they have come under the power of unwholesome friends, and through their evil deeds they will fall into the lower realms. From this day on we will not protect them. We will not guard the dwellings, the lands, the cities, or the realm of these caṇḍāla kṣatriyas and the rest.' So saying, all the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, deities and others grow weary of them, take no further interest in them, and abandon them.

"Therefore all those dwelling in the lands and cities of the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas and the rest who are vessels of the true Dharma and true fields of merit leave those lands and go away. And if some of them do remain, a wish to give them up arises, since they do not want them, and they have no thought of sheltering and protecting them. In this way the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, deities and others, and all those who are vessels of the true Dharma and fields of merit, have no thought of guarding and sheltering the caṇḍāla kṣatriya and his realm, his lands and his cities. Since they have no thought of guarding and sheltering them, at that time both internal enemies and external enemies rise up against the realm of that land. To seize the kingdom they harm and kill one another, and because of this, disease, famine and other calamities arise.

"Then all those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas through the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and all who live in that land, have no joy or gladness of heart. They are parted from everything that was once pleasing and dear to them. Relatives, companions and friends grow weary of one another and do not wish to meet. They have no loving-kindness and no compassion, but become jealous and miserly and commit many evil deeds: they engage in everything from killing living beings to holding wrong views, and, without shame or scruple, they use and consume the wealth of stūpas and the wealth of the Saṅgha, without any remorse. That caṇḍāla kṣatriya hates the wise and the upright and favors liars and deceivers, so the ministers and the common people harm one another, nurse grudges and resentment, and fight and quarrel. And when they do battle with a foreign enemy, because those below are not of one mind, they are defeated in battle."

"All those caṇḍālas, the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins and the rest, will before long suffer the decay of their limbs, and for many days and nights their tongues will be mute. They will experience the great suffering of extremely unbearable sickness and fever, and when they have died and passed on, they will without any doubt fall into the hell of Unceasing Torment.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, there are caṇḍālas, caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins and others, who follow evil friends. Their roots of virtue are slight, they live by deceit and guile, they are dull and deluded and conceited about their knowledge, they have no purity of intention toward the Three Jewels, and they do not regard the fruit of suffering in the next world with fear. Among them, some have a little faith in the Śrāvaka Vehicle, yet, being utterly dull by nature, they think, 'No one is more learned or wise than I.' So they despise and disparage, deride and slander the Dharma of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle and the Dharma of the Great Vehicle that I have taught, and they will not let other beings keep or hold, read or recite even so much as a single verse of them.

"Among them, some have gained a little faith in the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, yet, being utterly dull by nature, they think, 'No one is wiser or more learned than I.' So they despise and disparage, deride and slander the Dharma of the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Dharma of the Great Vehicle that I have taught, and they will not let other beings keep, hold, read or recite even so much as a single verse of them.

"Among them, some have gained just a little faith in the Dharma of the Great Vehicle, yet, being utterly dull by nature, they think, 'No one is more learned or wise than I.' So they despise and disparage, deride and slander the Dharma of the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Dharma of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle that I have taught, and they will not let other beings keep, hold, read or recite even so much as a single verse of them.

"Such persons are said to despise and slander the sacred Dharma of the buddhas, to lord it over the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the three times, to tear down all the collections of the Dharma of the buddhas of the three times, burning them and destroying them like ashes, to cut off the whole of the noble eightfold path, and to put out the eyes of countless beings.

"If those, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, conceal and obstruct the Dharma of the Śrāvaka Vehicle, the Dharma of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, and the Dharma of the Great Vehicle taught by the Buddha, so that not even a single verse remains, then such persons show disrespect to all who carry on the eye of the Dharma and the lineage of the Three Jewels. Through that cause and that condition, the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, deities and others who protect that land, whose minds are pure toward the Three Jewels and whose faith is unshakable, become angry with all those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla brahmins, and feelings of aversion arise in them; and so on, in full as in the passage above, until: all the caṇḍālas, the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins and the rest, will before long suffer the decay of their limbs, and for many days and nights their tongues will be mute. They will experience the great suffering of extremely unbearable sickness and fever, and when they have died and passed on, they will without any doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, the caṇḍālas, the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins and others, follow monks who have broken their moral discipline and commit evil; and so on, in full as in the passage above, until: all in that land who are vessels of the Dharma and true fields of merit form the intention to abandon the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas and the rest, and do not shelter or protect them. Even if by chance they remain living in that land, they rely on the Dharma and do not take pleasure in householders or stay with them. They do not go again and again to the homes of patrons, and if they do go for a moment, they guard their speech and keep silent; and if they do speak, there is no falsehood in it. In front of householders they do not deride or despise those who have broken their moral discipline. They do not declare or bring up the faults of those monks who have broken their moral discipline and commit evil. Always relying on the field of merit, they completely avoid those who have broken their moral discipline.

"Those monks who have broken their moral discipline and commit evil, in turn, become angry and form feelings of aversion toward those who keep moral discipline and possess wholesome qualities, and they despise and disparage them. In front of the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas through the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and in front of householder men and women, they tell lies with slander and all kinds of deceit, deriding and slandering those who keep moral discipline and heaping all kinds of abuse upon them. They cause all of them, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla brahmins, to feel aversion and anger toward my disciples, ascetics who have few desires and are content, who keep moral discipline, who are learned and possess eloquence; they make them deride and slander those ascetics with all kinds of harsh words, and so bring them distress of body and mind. Moreover, they have their robes, their alms bowls and their means of livelihood seized, so that they are deprived of a living. Moreover, they have the property of the Saṅgha of the four directions seized and do not allow them to use it. Some they have thrown into prison and put under the constraint of iron chains, fetters, wooden stocks and the like. Some have their limbs cut off, and some they have beheaded.

"Son of noble family, look! In this way those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, relying on monks who have broken their moral discipline and commit evil, commit all kinds of evil deeds of great wrongdoing such as these, and they will fall even into the hell of Unceasing Torment."

"The evil of any being who has committed the five deeds of immediate retribution, or destroyed the root precepts of moral discipline, or committed an evil close to the deeds of immediate retribution, or any evil by nature or evil by prescription, is lighter than the evil of those, from the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas to the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, who rely on those who have broken their moral discipline and act contrary to the Dharma.

"Son of noble family, although such monks who have broken their moral discipline and commit evil have thus committed great evil contrary to the Dharma, they nevertheless remain within my teaching. They have shaved their hair and beards and wear saffron robes, and their deportment and appearance accord with those of the noble ones. Kings, great ministers and householders are not permitted even to subject them to the laws of householders; how much less to beat them, to fine them, to put them under constraint, to throw them into prison, to abuse and revile them, to cut off their limbs, or to commit the unlawful act of severing their life faculty. If kings, ministers or householders were to do so, they would incur a great evil of wrongdoing and would without any doubt be born in the hell of Unceasing Torment. If one is not permitted to punish even monks who have broken their moral discipline and commit evil, how much less those who possess moral discipline and practice virtue!

"Son of noble family, though a monk who has broken even one of the root precepts of moral discipline is called a monk who has broken his moral discipline and commits evil, still the vow conferred through the formal act performed by a faithful Saṅgha, as the teaching prescribes, continues unbroken in him. Even if he has given up the training and moral discipline, something of the pure Dharma still remains with him, if only in name. Therefore kings, ministers, householders and those without vows are not permitted to despise or disparage him, or to punish him.

"Such a monk is indeed not a vessel of the Dharma. Because he has fallen from the noble Dharma, he defiles the pure Saṅgha and renders unfit all the formal acts that ascetics are to perform. He has no right to make use of the possessions of the Saṅgha of the four directions. Nevertheless, since he has not given up the vow he obtained when he received full ordination from a harmonious Saṅgha, he is superior to all householders. If this is so even for one who has broken a root precept, what need is there to speak of one who has broken some other minor prescribed precept? Therefore kings, ministers and householders have no right to despise him, disparage him, or punish him.

"Why is that? Son of noble family, in times long past, in the land called Kāśi, there was a king called Brahmadatta. He commanded five caṇḍālas, saying, 'Today, near the Snowy Mountains, there is an elephant with six tusks called Upāli's Eye. Pull out its tusks and bring them to me. If you do not get them, do not hope to stay alive!'

"Then, to save their bodies and their lives, those caṇḍālas each took up a bow and arrows, put on dyed robes, disguised themselves as ascetics, and went to the place near the Snowy Mountains where the king of the elephants was. When they arrived, a female elephant saw them first. Frightened by those men carrying bows and arrows, she ran to the king of the elephants and said, 'Great lord, do you not see? Some men are approaching, carrying bows and arrows and creeping slowly along. Surely they will not bring danger to our lives?'

"The king of the elephants raised his head and looked, and when he saw men coming with shaven heads and beards, wearing dyed robes, he spoke these verses to the female elephant:

'These who wear the robe, the victory banner
Of buddhas as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River,
Have completely given up all evil,
And so they surely do no harm to beings.'

"Then the female elephant also answered in verse:

'Though they make themselves look like wearers of the robe,
Since all of them carry bows and arrows in their hands,
These are pitiless and savage men,
Caṇḍālas bent on doing evil.'

"Then the king of the elephants spoke these verses:

'Since they appear wearing the patched robe,
They are the very root of loving-kindness and compassion.
Because they love and cherish all beings,
They have surely gone for refuge to the Buddha.
You should have no doubt about this,
But would do well to quickly take them to heart.
These who wear the dyed robes
Are the ones who free us from the ocean of saṃsāra.'

"Then at that moment those caṇḍālas shot a poisoned arrow at the king of the elephants, and it struck his heart, just as they had aimed. Then the female elephant let out a great cry and wept, and, her voice choked with sobs, she spoke these verses:

'Those who wear the robes,
I thought, had gone for refuge to the Buddha.
Though their appearance seemed so peaceful,
They nursed a vicious mind within.
To utterly destroy this enemy
Who shot an arrow at my lord's body,
I shall quickly trample his whole body
And sever him from his life faculty.'

"Then the king of the elephants spoke these verses:

'It is easier to be parted from the breath of life
Than it is right to give rise to an evil thought.
Though these have deceitful minds,
In appearance they are like the disciples of the Buddha.
The mind of the wise is perfectly pure;
Not for the sake of one's own life,
But in order to deliver many beings,
One always practices the conduct of awakening.'

"At that time the king of the elephants, his heart filled with tender love, said to those men, 'What do you want from me?' They said, 'We want your tusks.'"

"The king of the elephants, his heart filled with joy, pulled out his tusks, gave them to the men, and spoke this verse:

'Without any thought of anger or begrudging,
I give you these white tusks of mine.
Through the merit of this gift, may I attain perfect awakening
And bring peace to the sickness of beings' afflictions.'

"Son of noble family, look how in times past even that king of the elephants, though born in the animal realm, out of desire for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening gave up his body and life without attachment, and, in order to serve and honor those who wore the robe, did not retaliate even though they treated him as an enemy.

"In the future, the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics and caṇḍāla brahmins, though they are utterly dull by nature, will be conceited about their slight knowledge. They will live by deceit and guile and deceive the world, and since they do not regard the fruit of suffering in the next world with fear, they will harm and injure all those who have taken refuge in my teaching and gone forth, whether they are vessels of the Dharma or not. They will abuse and revile them, fine them, beat them, throw them into prison, and go so far as to sever them from their life faculty. In doing so they commit a great wrong that violates the commitment of the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past, the future and the present, and they will without any doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment. Since they have completely burned up their roots of virtue and cut off their continuity, all the wise should shun them entirely.

"Yet even though they have committed such great evil and wrongdoing, they live by deceit and guile, and, under the sway of pride, in order to deceive the world, each of them says, 'We too aspire to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. We shall attain full awakening through the Great Vehicle.'

"For example, it is impossible for a man who has gouged out his own eyes and become blind to climb a great mountain in order to show others the way. In the same way, in the future, at a later time, the caṇḍāla kṣatriyas, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics and caṇḍāla brahmins will be like that. They will harm and injure the disciples who have taken refuge in my teaching and gone forth, whether they are vessels of the Dharma or not; they will abuse and revile them, beat them, throw them into prison, and go so far as to sever them from their life faculty. Because they have thereby committed a great wrong that violates the commitment of all the buddhas of the past, the future and the present, they have burned up all their roots of virtue and cut off their continuity, and they will without any doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment. Therefore the wise should shun them. Yet even though they have committed such great wrongs, they live by deceit and guile, and, under the sway of pride, in order to deceive the world, they say, 'We are seeking unsurpassed awakening. We too shall attain full awakening through the Great Vehicle.' Because they have harmed and injured those who have gone forth in this way, they will not obtain even the body of the lowest of the low, let alone the awakening of the two vehicles or the unsurpassed vehicle. Their good fortune is cut off.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, in times long past, in the land called Pañcāla, there was a king called Victor over Foes, who held power and ruled the realm of that land. There was a great charnel ground there called Khalampa, a most terrifying place, where many yakṣas and rākṣasas dwelled. Anyone who went there would be terrified, and their hair would stand on end. In that land, whenever there was a criminal to be executed, the one condemned to death by the king's law would be bound with the five bonds and taken to that great charnel ground called Khalampa, where he would be left for the rākṣasas and bhūtas to eat.

"One condemned man, to save his life, shaved his hair and beard, and tied to his neck a scrap of a monk's robe that he had found. The jailer led him away, bound him with the five bonds according to the king's law, and took him to that great charnel ground. After those who had escorted him had turned back, that night a great rākṣasī called Weapon Eye arrived at the charnel ground with a retinue of five thousand. The man saw her from afar and was terrified and afraid. Then that rākṣasī saw the man bound with the five bonds, his hair and beard shaved, with a scrap of a monk's robe tied to his neck. She circled him, keeping him to her right, bowed to him with reverence, joined her palms, and spoke these verses:

'Sir, do not fear; remain at ease.
We will do you no harm.
Seeing you with shaven head and wearing the robe,
We have come to recollect the Buddha.'

"Then the rākṣasī's children said to their mother:

'We are so hungry and thirsty
That our bodies and minds are tormented.
To ease our bodies and minds,
Mother, please allow us to eat this man.'

"Then the rākṣasī said to her children:

'If one gives rise to unwholesome and evil thoughts
Toward one who wears the robe, the victory banner of liberation
Of buddhas as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River,
One will surely fall into the hell of Unceasing Torment.'

"Then the rākṣasī, together with her children and retinue, circled around him, bowed to him with reverence, joined her palms, and spoke these verses:

'Wearer of the dyed robe, we each confess before you.
It would be easier for us
To commit unwholesome deeds of body, speech and mind
Against our father and mother
Than ever to do you harm.'

"Then another great rākṣasī, called Donkey Tooth, arrived at the charnel ground with a retinue of five thousand. She too saw the man bound with the five bonds, his hair and beard shaved, with a scrap of a monk's robe tied to his neck. She circled him, bowed to him with reverence, joined her palms, and spoke these verses:

'Sir, I bid you, do not fear; remain at ease.
This scrap of cloth tied to your neck
Is the victory banner, the garment of the great sage.
To it we make offerings and bow.'

"Then that rākṣasī's children said to their mother:"

'Nothing is more delicious than human flesh and blood;
From it the strength of body and mind arises and grows,
Making one exceedingly brave, bold, and fearless.
Mother, we beg you, let us feast on him.'

"Then the rākṣasī said to her children:

'All the riches that gods and humans enjoy
Are obtained through those who have gone forth.
Therefore, by making offerings to this one who wears the Dharma robe,
One will attain happiness beyond measure.'

"Then the rākṣasī's children, together with their retinue, circled him, bowed to him with reverence, joined their palms, and spoke these verses:

'To this person with shaven head who wears the Dharma robe
We bow with the deepest reverence.
May we always, throughout all times to come,
See the buddhas and give rise to faith.'

"At that time another great rākṣasī, called Frowning Forelock, arrived at the charnel ground with a retinue of five thousand. She too saw the man bound with the five bonds, his hair and beard shaved, with a scrap of a monk's robe tied to his neck. She circled him to the right, bowed to him with reverence, joined her palms, and spoke these verses:

'This garment of the great sage, his victory banner,
Is highly praised and extolled by all the wise.
Whoever makes offerings to it
Will surely be freed from all the bonds of existence.'

"Then that rākṣasī's children said to their mother:

'All the flesh and blood of this man's body
The great king has dedicated to us.
Please let us eat and drink of him;
Grown strong, we will serve you, mother, as well.'

"Then the rākṣasī said to her children:

'Those who wear the Dharma robe, even one merely tied on,
Are not fit for you to eat.
If you give rise to unwholesome, evil thoughts toward him,
You will surely become vessels of great suffering.'

"Then the rākṣasī's children, together with their retinue, circled him, bowed to him with reverence, joined their palms, and spoke these verses:

'You wear the garment of the great sage
And have become a supreme field of merit.
Therefore, by our making offerings to you,
May we be freed from the bonds of existence.'

"At that time yet another great rākṣasī, called Weapon Face, arrived at the charnel ground with a retinue of five thousand. She too saw the man bound with the five bonds, his hair and beard shaved, with a scrap of a monk's robe tied to his neck. She circled him, bowed to him with reverence, joined her palms, and spoke these verses:

'Since you wear the Dharma robe tied upon you,
You are certain to attain the bliss of nirvāṇa.
So if we were to do you harm,
We fear that all the buddhas would condemn us.'

"Then that rākṣasī's children said to their mother:

'We always steal the vital luster of humans,
And likewise eat their flesh and drink their blood,
So that our form and our power may fully grow.
Please let us feast on this one as we please.'

"Then the rākṣasī said to her children:

'If you harm this one with hair and beard shaved off
Who wears the Dharma robe,
You will surely fall into the hell of Unceasing Torment
And long be a vessel of great suffering.'

"Then the rākṣasī, together with her children and retinue, circled him, bowed to him with reverence, joined their palms, and spoke these verses:

'We are terrified of the hells,
And so we will not harm your life.
We will untie these bonds and set you free;
May we be freed from all the sufferings of hell.'

"Then the rākṣasī mothers and their children, together with their retinues, gave rise to compassion, untied the man's bonds, asked his forgiveness, cheered him, and sent him back. Early the next morning the man came before the king and reported in detail all that had happened. When King Victor over Foes and his retinue heard this, they were filled with faith and struck with wonder. He made it law and decreed to the people of his land: 'From this day forth, if anyone in my land mocks or harms a disciple of the Buddha, whether one who keeps moral discipline, one whose discipline is broken, or, at the very least, one who has no moral discipline but has shaved hair and beard and wears saffron robes, all such people shall be put to death.' By that cause and condition many people, delighting in merit, gradually turned toward the teaching, and all the people of Jambudvīpa under that king's rule took refuge in the Three Jewels with sincere intention.

"Son of noble family, so it was that in the past even rākṣasīs born in the lineage of hungry ghosts, stealers of vital luster, eaters of flesh and blood, greedy for evil and without compassion, circled such a man without moral discipline, with hair and beard shaved and a scrap of a Dharma robe tied to his neck, showed him reverence, served him, bowed to him, made offerings to him, and praised him in verse, and had no thought of harming him. Yet in times to come, caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, caṇḍāla brahmins, and others, harboring malicious minds and devoid of compassion, will commit evil worse than yakṣas and rākṣasas. Foolish and deluded, cutting off their roots of virtue, they will show no faith or respect toward disciples who have gone forth in my teaching and who, with hair and beard shaved, wear saffron robes, whether they have become vessels of the Dharma or not. Instead they will harm them, revile and beat them, threaten them harshly, cast them into prison, and go so far as to deprive them of life. Such people contravene the great prohibition taught by all the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past, the future, and the present. Having utterly burned up their roots of virtue and cut off their continuity, they will without doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment. Therefore the wise should utterly avoid this.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, long ago there was a king called Great Merit. Because that king was greatly compassionate, he did not like to execute even those whose crimes had brought them to the point of execution. At that time a wise minister said to the king:

"'Your Majesty, do not be troubled. There is a way to put that man to death by which the king will not incur the evil of taking life, and the executioner too will not have to kill him.' Having said this, the great minister placed the man condemned to death before a vicious elephant in rut. The elephant seized the man with its trunk, lifted him up, and was about to dash him to the ground, when it saw that the man was wearing red cloth. Thinking, 'This is a Dharma robe,' it gave rise to faith with a pure mind and gently set him down on the ground. Then, regretting what it had done, it knelt to the ground, stroked the man's feet with its trunk, and with reverence gazed at him without blinking. When the great minister saw what had happened, he ran to the king and told him all of it. Hearing this, the king was startled and struck with wonder, and he decreed to all his people that from that day forth they must show reverence to the Three Jewels. By that cause and condition he also gave up taking life, and came to rule over Jambudvīpa.

"See, son of noble family: in the past even an elephant in rut, born among animals, did no evil, out of respect for the Dharma robe. Yet in times to come caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, caṇḍāla brahmins, and others, with malicious minds and no compassion, will commit evil deeds worse than that elephant in rut. Those childish fools will show no faith or respect toward those who have gone forth in my teaching, whether they have become vessels of the Dharma or not, who have shaved their hair and beard and wear saffron robes. Instead they will harm them, revile and abuse them, beat and strike them, cast them into prison, and go so far as to deprive them of life. By this they contravene the great prohibition taught by all the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past, the future, and the present. Having burned up their roots of virtue and cut off their continuity, they will without doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment. Therefore the wise should avoid this.

"If caṇḍāla kings, and so on up to caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, possess these three wheels of evil, then by that cause and condition all the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, and others who protect the land, whose intention toward the Three Jewels is pure and whose faith is unshakable, will give rise to anger and hostility toward those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kings up to the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and so on, as given in full in the text before. All those caṇḍālas, the caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins, and the rest, will before long have the limbs of their bodies cut to pieces, will be struck dumb for many days, and will experience the great suffering of utterly unbearable sickness and fever. And when they die and pass on, they will fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, in times to come, here in this buddha field, there will be caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, caṇḍāla brahmins, and others who rely on evil friends, whose roots of virtue are slight and weak, and so on, as given in full before, and who do not see the suffering of the world beyond. They will seize the property of the Saṅgha of the four directions, the property of monasteries, property dedicated to monasteries, parks, property dedicated to parks, fields and pastures, and property dedicated to fields and pastures. They will seize the men and women who serve as attendants of the various monasteries; they will seize wealth and livestock, clothing and alms food, mats and seats, and medicine for the sick, and they will seize all the various other requisites of life. The property thus offered to the Saṅgha of the four directions is to be granted solely for the use of pure monks: those endowed with moral discipline and good qualities, who exert diligence, who earnestly apply themselves on the paths of training and of no more training, up to those who have attained the fruit of reaching the final state. Yet those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kings up to the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, puffed up with their might and their armies, will seize it by force. They will not let pure monks endowed with moral discipline use it, but will give it to monks whose discipline is broken, who do evil, who live in households and do the work of householders. Some of them use it in common; some use it privately for themselves. And monks of broken discipline, hearing of this, likewise use it, some in common and some privately for themselves, while some give it to householders and share it with them. By that cause and condition the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, and others who protect the land, whose intention toward the Three Jewels is pure and whose faith is unshakable, become angry and give rise to hostility toward those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kings up to the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and so on, as given in full before. Those caṇḍālas, the caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins, and the rest, will before long have the limbs of their bodies cut to pieces, will be struck dumb for many days, and will experience the great suffering of utterly unbearable sickness and fever. And when they die and pass on, they will without doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, in times to come, here in this buddha field, there will be caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, caṇḍāla brahmins, and others who rely on evil friends, whose roots of virtue are slight and weak, and so on, as given in full before, and who do not see the fruit of suffering in the world beyond. They will revile and abuse, disparage, mock, slander, oppress, and harm a Dharma preacher who has gone forth in my teaching, who is sharp in wisdom and greatly learned, whose speech is perfect in words and meaning, who is able to teach the Dharma of the Śrāvaka Vehicle, or who has realized and is able to teach the Dharma of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, or who is able to teach the Dharma of the Unsurpassed Vehicle and to teach it extensively for the benefit and happiness of beings; and they will obstruct his teaching of the Dharma.

"By that cause and condition the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, and others who protect the land, whose intention toward the Three Jewels is pure and whose faith is unshakable, become angry and give rise to hostility toward those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kings up to the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and so on, as given in full in the text before. All those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kings to the ministers, householders, ascetics, brahmins, and the rest, will before long have their own bodies cut to pieces, will be struck dumb for many days, and will experience the great suffering of utterly unbearable sickness and fever. And when they die and pass on, they will without doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, in times to come, here in this buddha field, there will be caṇḍālas, caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins, and others, who rely on evil friends, whose roots of virtue are weak and slight, and so on, as given in full before, and who do not look with fear upon the fruit of suffering in the world beyond. They will seize the property offered to the Saṅgha of the four directions: the fields and pastures of the monasteries, wealth and livestock, jewels, flowering trees, fruit trees, trees for dyes, shade trees, fragrant trees, medicinal trees, and the like, and all the various other requisites of life. Such things are to be used only by my disciples, the pure monks endowed with moral discipline and good qualities, who exert diligence, who earnestly apply themselves on the paths of training and of no more training, up to those close to attaining the fruit of reaching the final state. Yet caṇḍāla kings, caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and others, puffed up with their might and their armies, seize these things by force, or have others seize them, or use them themselves, or let others use them. By that cause and condition the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, and others who protect the land, whose intention toward the Three Jewels is pure and whose faith is unshakable, become angry and give rise to hostility toward the caṇḍāla kings, caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and the rest, and so on, as given in full before. All those caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, caṇḍāla brahmins, and the rest will before long have the limbs of their bodies cut to pieces, will be struck dumb for many days, and will experience the great suffering of utterly unbearable sickness and fever. And when they die and pass on, they will without doubt fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, in times to come, here in this buddha field, there will be caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, caṇḍāla brahmins, and others who rely on evil friends, whose roots of virtue are slight and weak, whose intentions are impure, who live by deceit and guile, who are foolish and deluded, proud of a mere smattering of knowledge, without truth in their words, who reject spiritual friends, rely on evil companions, and practice what is unwholesome, who doubt the Dharma of the noble ones, and who do not look with fear upon the fruit of suffering in the world beyond. They delight in keeping vows of evil, are drawn to everything from taking life to wrong view, are possessed by pride, and deceive worldly people. These caṇḍāla kings, caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and others, who claim, 'I abide in the vows of moral discipline,' will destroy my teaching by all sorts of means. Again and again they will rage at those who have gone forth in my teaching, revile them, abuse and disparage them, oppress them harshly, cast them into prison, cut off their limbs, and go so far as to deprive them of life. Having no faith in the Dharma I have taught, they destroy stūpas, destroy monasteries, force monks back into lay life, and obstruct people from shaving their hair and beard and putting on saffron robes; they force those who have gone forth to do all kinds of work and make them their servants. By that cause and condition the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, and others who protect the land, whose intention toward the Three Jewels is pure and whose faith is unshakable, become angry and give rise to hostility toward the caṇḍāla kings and the caṇḍāla ascetics and brahmins, and so on, as given in full before. Those caṇḍālas, the caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins, and the rest, will before long have all the limbs of their bodies cut to pieces, will be struck dumb for many days, and will experience the great suffering of utterly unbearable sickness and fever. And when they die and pass on, they will without doubt fall into the hell of Unceasing Torment.

"Son of noble family, if these caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins, and others possess even some of these ten kinds of wheels of evil that have been described, all the roots of virtue they accumulated before will be burned up like ashes and vanish. Before long the limbs of their bodies will be cut to pieces, they will be struck dumb for many days, and they will experience the great suffering of utterly unbearable sickness and fever. And when they die, they will fall into the great hell of Unceasing Torment. In times to come, it will be hard for these caṇḍāla kings, ministers, householders, merchants, ascetics, brahmins, and others even to obtain the body of the lowest and most wretched of humans, let alone to attain the awakening of the two vehicles or unsurpassed awakening; they are utterly without the fortune for it. When it is hard for such evil ones even to hear the name of the Great Vehicle, what need is there to speak of their attaining the fruit of unsurpassed, complete awakening? Because such a person has for a long time made himself unfit and has made others unfit as well, even all the buddhas are unable to protect him.

"Son of noble family, consider this: when someone works at pressing oil from seeds, each and every seed has become a living creature, and the oil comes out only when they are crushed. Look at how many beings that oil presser kills in a single day! Suppose such a seed-oil presser set up ten oil-pressing places, and in each of them pressed a thousand loads of seed every day without interruption for a full thousand years. Would that person's harmful deeds of taking life be very many?"

The bodhisattva, the great being Kṣitigarbha, replied, "Very many, Blessed One, very many! Sugata, that person's killing is beyond measure and beyond reckoning, and his harmful deeds cannot be weighed, counted, numbered, or even illustrated by example. The Tathāgata alone knows them; no one else does."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, consider this: suppose some man, for the sake of wealth and profit, set up ten brothels, placed a thousand prostitutes in each of them, adorned each of those women with all kinds of ornaments, and had them entice people and engage in acts of lust without interruption for a thousand years. That person's harmful evil would be immeasurable, beyond reckoning, counting, or even illustration by example. The evil of the ten seed-oil-pressing places equals the evil of setting up a single brothel.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, consider this: suppose some person, for the sake of wealth and profit, set up ten taverns, adorned each of them lavishly with all kinds of ornaments, and in each of them, by all sorts of means, lured in a thousand people fond of liquor, plied them with drink, and made merry day and night without interruption for a thousand years. That person's evil too would be immeasurable, beyond reckoning, counting, or even illustration by example. The evil of the ten brothels described above equals the evil of setting up a single tavern.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, consider this: suppose some person, for the sake of wealth and profit, set up ten slaughterhouses, and in each of them every day slaughtered a thousand each of cattle, sheep, camels, deer, fowl, and pigs, without interruption for a thousand years. That person's harmful evil too would be immeasurable, beyond reckoning, counting, or even illustration by example. Thus the evil of the ten taverns described before equals the evil of setting up a single slaughterhouse. And the evil of those ten slaughterhouses is equal to the harmful evil of a single day for those caṇḍālas, from the caṇḍāla kings up to the caṇḍāla ascetics, brahmins, and the rest, if they possess even some of the ten wheels of evil."

Then the Blessed One spoke these verses:

"The evil of ten oil-pressing places
Equals the evil of a single brothel.
The evil of ten brothels
Equals the evil of a single tavern.
Ten taverns
Equal the evil of a single slaughterhouse.
Ten slaughterhouses set up
Equal the evil of a single caṇḍāla king."

The fifth fascicle.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Kṣitigarbha, said to the Blessed One, "Venerable Blessed One, there may be virtuous kings, virtuous ministers, virtuous householders, virtuous merchants, virtuous ascetics, and virtuous brahmins who take good care of themselves and take good care of others as well, who are wary of the world beyond, and who abide in the teaching of the Buddha. With the deepest reverence they serve and make offerings to those who have gone forth, whether they have become vessels of the Dharma or have not become vessels of the Dharma, or, at the very least, are without moral discipline but have shaved their hair and beard and wear saffron robes. Moreover, with reverence they make offerings to the Dharma of the Śrāvaka Vehicle, the Dharma of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, and the Dharma of the Great Vehicle, listen to it, retain it, and honor it. They protect persons who abide in the Great Vehicle, who are endowed with moral discipline and good qualities, who exert diligence and apply themselves earnestly, up to those who abide in the fruit, and they lend them the support of their power. They ask them questions and inquire of them, listen to them and retain what they hear, and converse and talk with them gladly. They keep far away from monks of broken discipline who do evil. They protect the property of the Saṅgha of the four directions carefully and do not let outsiders squander it. They do not themselves steal the property of the Saṅgha of the four directions or the property of stūpas, nor have others steal it; they do not themselves make use of it, nor have others make use of it. With reverence they honor preachers of the Dharma of the three vehicles and lend them the support of their power; they do not let outsiders mock, slander, or disparage them, but serve them and encourage them. Out of faith they protect and care for those who have gone forth and the Dharma of the Buddha's teaching. They do not destroy stūpas, and they carefully protect the dwellings of the Saṅgha and the monasteries. They neither despise nor disparage persons who have gone forth, who have shaved hair and beard and wear saffron robes. They themselves do not engage in the ten kinds of wheels of evil, and they keep others far away from the ten wheels of evil. They govern the kingdom according to the Dharma and customs of the kings of old; they carry on the lineage of the Three Jewels, have faith in it, and make it flourish. They always rely on spiritual friends and care for all the people with a mind of loving-kindness, and they guide and teach everyone by whatever Dharma will tame each of them.

"And they lead others to give up wrong views and apply themselves earnestly to the true Dharma. How much merit will those virtuous ones, from the virtuous kings up to the virtuous brahmins, obtain, and how much evil will they be purified of?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, suppose a single person of great power appeared in the world. In the morning he filled the whole of Jambudvīpa with the seven kinds of precious things and offered them to the Buddha and the Saṅgha; at noon he again filled the whole of Jambudvīpa with the seven kinds of precious things and offered them to the Buddha and the Saṅgha; and in the evening he again filled the whole of Jambudvīpa with the seven kinds of precious things and offered them to the Buddha and the Saṅgha. If he gave such gifts in this way every single day without interruption for a hundred thousand years, would that person's accumulation of merit be great?"

The bodhisattva, the great being Kṣitigarbha, replied, "Exceedingly great, Blessed One, exceedingly great! Sugata, that person's accumulation of merit is beyond measure and beyond reckoning; it cannot be expressed, reckoned, counted, or even illustrated by example. The Buddha alone knows it; no one else does."
