These vows and this moral discipline of the prātimokṣa are the common wheel of a bodhisattva's pure moral discipline; they are shared with all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. By virtue of this pure wheel of a bodhisattva's moral discipline, the afflictions of all sentient beings are not cut off, their wrong views are not dispelled, and they are not freed from the obscuration of karma and from saṃsāra. So it is not called the wheel of great armor, nor is one called a bodhisattva great being, for one is not a true field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

What, then, is the uncommon wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's pure moral discipline? It is that bodhisattvas guard pure moral discipline with a mind of equality, a mind free of harm and a mind free of resentment toward all sentient beings of the ten directions. They guard moral discipline completely with a mind that makes no distinctions and a perception that sees no difference among all, whether superior, middling or inferior: those with moral discipline and those of bad discipline, the generous and the stingy, the loving and the hateful, the diligent and the lazy. They guard moral discipline completely free of aversion, free of anger and free of wrongdoing toward all sentient beings of the three realms. They guard moral discipline completely without conceptualizing the aggregates, elements and sense sources of the three kinds of existence. They guard moral discipline without dwelling in the desire realm. They guard moral discipline without dwelling in the form realm. They guard moral discipline without dwelling in the formless realm. They guard moral discipline without dwelling in any of the ripened fruits of existence. They guard moral discipline without dwelling in gain or loss. They guard moral discipline without dwelling in formations. These are called the uncommon wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's pure moral discipline.

Son of good family, the bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel of the great armor of pure moral discipline, from the first generation of the mind of awakening, entirely clears away the five sense pleasures and is called a bodhisattva great being. He far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

Taking vows while living in the household,
And the prātimokṣa discipline of those gone forth,
Are shared with the two vehicles,
And are not called the great awakening.

The wise who meditate on the Dharma of emptiness
Do not rely on or dwell in any world.
Without dwelling in any existence,
They guard pure moral discipline.

The moral discipline that abandons signs
Is free of attachment and free of all defilements.
Those who guard moral discipline such as this
Are called a true field of merit.

Furthermore, son of good family, the bodhisattva great being has what is called the wheel of the great armor of patience. The bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel, from the first generation of the mind of awakening, entirely clears away the five sense pleasures, far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of patience? Son of good family, a bodhisattva's patience has two characteristics. What are they? The worldly and the world-transcending.

What, then, is a bodhisattva's worldly patience? It is patience with defilements: one is patient while taking sentient beings as the object, with grasping, with signs, relying on the ripened fruit, and relying on meritorious action. Further, one is patient in dependence on one's own forms, sounds, smells, tastes and objects of touch. Patience bound up with involvement, patience that is not free of being troubled, patience of weak strength, patience that abandons sentient beings, patience accompanied by deceit and guile, patience that beguiles others, and patience that does not work for the benefit and happiness of sentient beings: these are called a bodhisattva's worldly patience. This patience is not called the wheel of great armor that is not shared with the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the rest. How so? Such a one is not a bodhisattva great being, nor is he called a true field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's world-transcending patience? It is undefiled patience. Through the great light of the Dharma of all the noble ones, for the sake of the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings, one practices a patience free of attachment, and a patience based on the sense source in which all objects of action, all verbal expression, causes, signs, letters, and all sounds and speech are completely cut off. By cultivating this patience, the three kinds of total affliction, the three kinds of eon, the three characteristics, the three times, the three existences, the three kinds of formations, the three roots of nonvirtue, the four floods, the four knots, the four kinds of grasping, the four bodily ties and the rest are completely cut off; and so, when one cultivates this patience, body and mind become utterly peaceful. This is called the patience of the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva that transcends the world.

Son of good family, the bodhisattva great being who possesses that patience of the wheel of great armor is called a bodhisattva great being because from the first generation of the mind of awakening he has entirely cleared away the five sense pleasures. He far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

Patience is of two kinds:
With signs and without signs.
Patience with signs involves attachment;
The wise do not praise it.

Cultivating patience that relies on
The three kinds of formations, the aggregates, the elements and the sense sources,
Is patience with defilements,
And is not that of a great bodhisattva.

Having cleared away the four inversions,
One cultivates patience free of attachment.
When the three kinds of formations are pacified,
All will come to praise this patience.

All formations utterly pacified,
All conceptions entirely abandoned,
The mind even like the sky:
All will come to praise this patience.

All dharmas agreeing in a single taste,
Empty, signless and at peace,
The mind dwelling in nothing and clinging to nothing:
This patience becomes of great benefit.

Furthermore, son of good family, the bodhisattva great being has a wheel of the great armor of diligence. The bodhisattva great being who possesses that wheel, from the first generation of the mind of awakening, entirely clears away the five sense pleasures, far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of diligence? Son of good family, a bodhisattva's diligence is of two kinds: worldly and world-transcending.

What, then, is a bodhisattva's worldly diligence? With diligence of intense effort, bodhisattvas devote themselves to the three worldly bases of meritorious action. What is merit? It is this: the meritorious action of giving, the action of making merit by guarding moral discipline, and the action of making merit through meditation. Performing these actions is called the three kinds of diligence. These three kinds of diligence take sentient beings as their object, are accompanied by defilements, are accompanied by grasping, rely on the ripened fruit and rely on meritorious action. This is called a bodhisattva's worldly diligence. This diligence is shared with the Śrāvaka Vehicle, the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle and the rest, and so it is not called the wheel of great armor, nor is one called a bodhisattva great being, for one is not a true field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's world-transcending diligence? Bodhisattvas, with diligence of intense effort and a mind of equality toward all sentient beings, clear away all afflicted action and suffering. This diligence is praised by all the noble ones; it is undefiled, free of grasping, without support and without abode. With a mind that makes no distinctions and a perception that sees no difference among all, whether superior, middling or inferior beings, the diligent and the lazy, the generous and the stingy, those with moral discipline and those of bad discipline, the loving and the hateful, they undertake diligence of intense effort. Toward all sentient beings of the three realms, they do the work to be done with equality and without an object, and with a mind that does not dwell in any ground of speech, conception or analysis they undertake diligence of intense effort. Without conceptualizing the aggregates, elements and sense sources of the three kinds of existence, they undertake diligence of intense effort: the diligent effort that does not dwell in the desire realm, the diligent effort that does not dwell in the form realm, the diligent effort that does not dwell in the formless realm, the diligent effort that does not conceptualize any of the ripened fruits of existence, the diligent effort that does not dwell in gain or loss, the diligent effort that does not dwell in formations, the diligent effort that does not dwell in the three worldly bases of meritorious action, and intense diligence undertaken in the three bases of meritorious action that are bound up with what transcends the world. This is called the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's world-transcending diligence.

Son of good family, the bodhisattva great being who possesses that wheel of the great armor of diligence, having entirely cleared away the five kinds of sense pleasure from the first generation of the mind of awakening, is called a bodhisattva great being. He far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

Fools deeply attached to the six faculties
Are carried away by the five rivers.
Though they undertake diligence of intense effort,
The wise will find fault with it.

If one undertakes diligence that takes beings as its object,
That is accompanied by defilements
And accompanied by grasping,
One is not a true field of merit,
And not a great bodhisattva.

The wise who undertake diligence
Have cast far away all attachment;
Their minds dwell in nothing and cling to nothing.
They are called a true field of merit.

Free of attachment to name and form,
Having abandoned the aggregates, elements and sense sources,
One who becomes the support of the whole assembly
Is a great bodhisattva.

Though moving in the world, he is like the moon in water;
He has perfected the undertaking of diligent effort.
By this wheel are utterly destroyed
The bonds of affliction that bind all people.

Furthermore, son of good family, the bodhisattva great being has a wheel of the great armor of meditative concentration. The bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel, from the first generation of the mind of awakening, entirely clears away the five sense pleasures, far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of meditative concentration? Son of good family, a bodhisattva's meditative concentration is of two kinds: worldly and world-transcending.

What, then, is a bodhisattva's worldly meditative concentration? It is that bodhisattvas cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in the aggregates, cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in the elements, and cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in the sense sources. They cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in the desire realm, while dwelling in the form realm, and while dwelling in the formless realm. They cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in the three vows, while dwelling in the three liberations, while dwelling in the four applications of mindfulness, while dwelling in the four right efforts, while dwelling in the four bases of miraculous power, while dwelling in the five faculties, while dwelling in the five powers, while dwelling in the seven branches of awakening, and while dwelling in the noble eightfold path. They cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in the earth element, while dwelling in the water element, while dwelling in the fire element, while dwelling in the wind element, while dwelling in the space element, and while dwelling in the consciousness element. They cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in pleasant feeling, while dwelling in painful feeling, and while dwelling in feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. They cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in the sphere of infinite space, while dwelling in the sphere of infinite consciousness, while dwelling in the sphere of nothingness, and while dwelling in the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception. They cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in this world and while dwelling in the world beyond. They cultivate meditative concentration while dwelling in limited perception, while dwelling in vast perception, and while dwelling in immeasurable perception. These meditative concentrations are accompanied by defilements, accompanied by grasping and accompanied by attachment; this is called a bodhisattva's worldly meditative concentration. This meditative concentration is said to be shared with the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle. It is not the wheel of great armor, and one is not called a bodhisattva great being, for one is not a true field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

What, then, is a bodhisattva's world-transcending meditative concentration? It is this: bodhisattvas cultivate meditative concentration free of the aggregates, cultivate meditative concentration free of the elements, and cultivate meditative concentration free of the sense sources. They cultivate meditative concentration free of the desire realm, free of the form realm, and free of the formless realm. They cultivate meditative concentration free of the three vows, free of the three liberations, free of the four applications of mindfulness, free of the four right efforts, free of the four bases of miraculous power, free of the five faculties, free of the five powers, free of the seven branches of awakening, and free of the noble eightfold path. They cultivate meditative concentration free of the earth element, free of the water element, free of the fire element, free of the wind element, free of the space element, and free of the consciousness element. They cultivate meditative concentration free of pleasant feeling, free of painful feeling, and free of feeling that is neither painful nor pleasant. They cultivate meditative concentration free of the sphere of infinite space, free of the sphere of infinite consciousness, free of the sphere of nothingness, and free of the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception. They cultivate meditative concentration free of this world and free of the world beyond. They cultivate meditative concentration free of limited perception, free of vast perception, and free of immeasurable perception. This meditative concentration gives rise to the great light of the noble ones; it is undefiled, free of grasping and free of attachment. This is called the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's world-transcending meditative concentration.

Son of good family, the bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel of the great armor of meditative concentration, having entirely cleared away the five sense pleasures from the first generation of the mind of awakening, is called a bodhisattva great being. He far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

To cast off one's own heavy burden,
One cultivates absorption that has an object,
Wishing to cut off one's own afflictions:
This is not the mark of the truly wise.

Relying on a support and accompanied by conception,
Though one cultivates absorption, wishing for liberation,
If one dwells at the edge of this shore or the far shore,
One does not work for the benefit of beings.

For the benefit and happiness of all beings
One cultivates absorption and casts off the heavy burden,
Clearing away all the afflictions:
That is the mark of the truly wise.

To bring refreshment to all beings,
One cultivates the absorption that does not abide,
Cutting off all existence in the realm of desire:
Such a one is called a person of great intelligence.

To free all who are bound by existence
And bring them into the city of fearlessness,
One cultivates utterly peaceful absorption:
Such a one is a great bodhisattva.

The tenth and final fascicle.

Furthermore, son of good family, the bodhisattva great being has what is called the wheel of the great armor of wisdom. The bodhisattva great being who possesses that wheel, from the first generation of the mind of awakening, entirely clears away the five sense pleasures, far surpasses all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all who follow the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of wisdom? Son of good family, a bodhisattva's wisdom is of two kinds: worldly and world-transcending.

What, then, is a bodhisattva's worldly wisdom? It is this: bodhisattvas rely solely on reading the true Dharma of the three vehicles, reciting it, copying it into books, listening to it, and teaching and explaining it to others. Wishing to dispel the darkness of ignorance of all sentient beings, and so that the lamp of wisdom may arise for all sentient beings, with diligence of intense effort they read, recite, listen to, copy into books, and teach and explain extensively to others the Dharma connected with the Śrāvaka Vehicle taught by the Tathāgata, and devote themselves to it with true diligence. They read, recite, listen to, copy into books, and teach and explain extensively to others the Dharma connected with the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle taught by the Tathāgata, and practice it devotedly with true diligence. With diligence they read, recite, listen to, copy into books, and teach and explain extensively to others as well the Dharma connected with the Great Vehicle taught by the Tathāgata, and devote themselves to it with true diligence. Yet they do not seek the branches of the undefiled noble path, do not seek the noble path, do not seek the liberation that belongs to the noble path, and do not devote themselves to the utterly peaceful true wisdom; rather, they always devote themselves to a wisdom that is accompanied by views and accompanied by the notion of wisdom. Such wisdom, accompanied by grasping and accompanied by attachment, is called a bodhisattva's worldly wisdom. That wisdom is shared with all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and so it is not called the wheel of great armor, nor is such a one called a bodhisattva great being, for he is not a true field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

What, then, is a bodhisattva's transcendent wisdom? It is this. Bodhisattvas who apply themselves with diligence to the path of awakening read, recite and listen to the true Dharma of the three vehicles as far as they are able, copy it into volumes, and teach and explain it extensively to others; yet they abide in a practice that does not take it as an object. They are accomplished with a mind that is unmoving, nonconceptual and without essential nature, a mind like space, a mind utterly at peace, a wisdom without increase or decrease, a mind without attachment, a mind without arising and cessation, an irreversible mind, a mind of the equality of all phenomena, a mind of suchness, a mind of the limit of reality, a mind of the realm of phenomena, a mind of selflessness, a mind free of conceptualization, utterly peaceful and patient, free of conceptual thought. Through supreme skillful means they dwell at ease on the ground of fearlessness, and through skillful means they dwell at ease on the ground of the supreme and sublime intelligence that neither abides nor clings. Because this wisdom is without grasping and without attachment, it is called the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's transcendent wisdom.

Son of good family, when a bodhisattva great being possesses this wheel of the great armor of wisdom, then, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, because he has eliminated the five sense pleasures he is called a bodhisattva great being. He passes far beyond the grounds of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is one to be worshiped, protected and served by all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

Furthermore, son of good family, there is the bodhisattva great being's wheel of the great armor of skillful means. A bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, eliminates the five sense pleasures, passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; all followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle and the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle will worship, protect and serve him.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of skillful means? Son of good family, a bodhisattva's skillful means is of two kinds: worldly and transcendent.

What is a bodhisattva's worldly skillful means? It is this. For their own benefit, for the benefit of others, or for the benefit of both, bodhisattvas think in terms of self and other, and, in order to accomplish fully the benefit of self and others through many kinds of crafts and arts, they make offerings to and serve the buddhas, the blessed ones, bodhisattvas, pratyekabuddhas and śrāvakas, and their fathers and mothers. When they see the sick, the destitute, those without protector or refuge, and those suffering from many harms, they rescue them with supreme skillful means and bring beings to full maturity through the means of attraction.

That bodhisattva, himself abiding in the Great Vehicle, teaches the very subtle and profound true Dharma to followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle and followers of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas who are not vessels for the Great Vehicle and whose faculties are not fully matured; he sets them to train and has them devote themselves to the meditative concentrations of the noble ones. Furthermore, he teaches them the ultimate truth and has them devote themselves to it; he turns them back from the four distortions and has them understand the characteristics of the phenomena by which one does not fall; he has them apply themselves to the four kinds of discriminating knowledge; and further, through the wisdom that is skilled in leading them into the four applications of mindfulness, the four correct exertions, the four bases of miraculous power, the five faculties, the five powers, the seven factors of awakening, the noble eightfold path, and the path with remainder and without remainder, up to the fruits of the path, he brings them to full maturity.

Beings who are attached to gain, fame and wealth, whose faculties are restless, and whose roots of virtue are not fully matured, he brings to full maturity by having them read and recite the sūtras, the Vinaya and the Mātṛkā, and by having them read and recite treatises other than those the Buddha taught that accord with liberation. Beings who have no inclination to give, he brings to full maturity by urging them to give away jewels and wealth and riches of many kinds. Beings who are fierce and of unstable nature he urges to train in the four abodes of Brahmā; beings full of anger he urges to train in patience; lazy beings he urges to train in diligence; beings whose minds are distracted he urges to cultivate meditative concentration; and beings whose wisdom is corrupt he brings to full maturity with skillful means, teaching them the true Dharma, instructing them and conferring scriptural transmission on them. Beings who have no faith in the Three Jewels and neither abide in nor practice them, he brings to full maturity by having them go for refuge to the Three Jewels, have faith in the Three Jewels, train in the vow of a layman, and train in the vow of the one-day fast. Furthermore, he brings them to full maturity by having them learn and practice the various fields of crafts and arts.

The forms of such supremely skillful worldly wisdom of a bodhisattva great being are more numerous than the grains of sand in the river Ganges. Thus, to subdue all non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) and all who follow other teachers through superior conduct, diligence, and the wisdom of skillful means in every field of writing, treatises, crafts and arts: that is a bodhisattva's worldly skillful means.

This skillful means is shared with all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. It has become the cause on which all the qualities of a buddha rest and the ground for the practice of skillful means; being supremely skillful in means and spontaneously accomplished, it is free of conceptual thought, and so it has become a quality that does not turn back.

Furthermore, son of good family, if a bodhisattva, relying neither on a learned teacher nor looking to a spiritual friend, applies himself to worldly skillful means, that bodhisattva is deluded by worldly skillful means and will fall into the lower realms. He is unable to apply himself to and abide in the wisdom of transcendent, supreme skillful means, and he is not a true field of merit for anyone. Lacking supreme skill in means, he does not know the differences in the faculties and conduct of beings; and being deluded about supreme skillful means, he teaches the Great Vehicle to those who are not vessels for the Śrāvaka Vehicle, the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle or the Great Vehicle, and to those whose faculties are not matured for the Great Vehicle, and he has them train in it and devote themselves to it. To beings who have become vessels for the Dharma of the Great Vehicle, he teaches the Dharma of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles, and has them train in and devote themselves to the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles. To beings who have become vessels for the Dharma of the Buddha Vehicle, he teaches the Dharma of the Śrāvaka Vehicle, and has them train in and devote themselves to the Śrāvaka Vehicle. To beings who have become vessels for the Dharma of the Śrāvaka Vehicle, he presents the things of saṃsāra as happiness and joy, and does not teach them so as to produce weariness with the things of saṃsāra.

Furthermore, being deluded about supreme skillful means, to beings who delight in killing, and so on up to those inclined to wrong views, he teaches the very profound Dharma of the Great Vehicle; he does not teach them weariness and fear at dying and passing from this round of existence, being born in the next, and experiencing the ripened fruit of suffering, nor does he teach them to give up sinful, nonvirtuous things. Furthermore, being deluded about supreme skillful means, he has beings who are inclined to keep moral discipline practice generosity. To beings inclined to cultivate patience he teaches the abandonment of patience and has them keep moral discipline. To beings inclined to cultivate diligence he teaches the abandonment of diligence and has them practice patience. To beings inclined to cultivate meditative concentration he teaches the abandonment of meditative concentration and has them devote themselves to diligence. To beings inclined to cultivate wisdom he teaches the abandonment of wisdom and has them cultivate meditative concentration.

Thus a bodhisattva deluded about worldly skillful means is unable to work for the benefit and happiness of beings correctly, just as things are, and becomes an evil friend to all beings. This skillful means is accompanied by objectification and accompanied by attachment; it is called a bodhisattva's worldly skillful means. Thus this worldly skillful means is shared with the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles. It is not the wheel of great armor, nor is such a one called a bodhisattva great being, for he is not a true field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

What, then, is a bodhisattva's transcendent, supreme skillful means? It is this. Bodhisattvas give up their own benefit and make the benefit of others their chief concern; in order to bring beings to maturity by displaying many kinds of crafts and arts, they serve and make offerings to the buddhas, the blessed ones, bodhisattvas, śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and their fathers and mothers. Those who are sick, in want, destitute, without protector or refuge, and tormented by many kinds of intense suffering, they rescue through skillful means, and they bring beings to full maturity through the four means of attraction. They teach the true Dharma to beings according to their inclinations, their faculties and their capacity as vessels.

Furthermore, step by step, they urge followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle to train in and devote themselves to the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, and urge followers of the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle to train in and devote themselves to the Great Vehicle. If there are those whose faculties are not matured for the Śrāvaka or Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, they teach them weariness through the painful nature of saṃsāra, and urge them to grow weary of saṃsāra and to seek nirvāṇa. To beings who are inclined to killing, and so on up to those attached to wrong views, they teach, in accordance with their faculties and nature, how one circles in saṃsāra; they teach that one is born from this world into the next and experiences in the next the fruit of suffering, and so teach fear of and weariness with sinful, nonvirtuous things. To some they teach the Dharma connected with the Śrāvaka Vehicle; to some they teach the Dharma connected with the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle; to some they fully reveal the Dharma of the unsurpassed vehicle and lead them gradually to train in it. To beings who are inclined to generosity they teach the keeping of supreme, perfectly pure moral discipline; and so on in full, likewise, to beings inclined to meditative concentration they teach what is comprised by the supreme undefiled path of the noble ones and unsurpassed wisdom, and have them devote themselves to it.

This supreme skillful means is without objectification and without attachment; it is called the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's transcendent skillful means. Son of good family, a bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel of the great armor of supreme skillful means, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, because he has eliminated the five kinds of sense pleasure, is called a bodhisattva great being. He passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is one to be worshiped, protected and served by all followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

"Devotion to wisdom is of two kinds,
Worldly and beyond the world.
That with attachment is worldly;
That without attachment is beyond the world.

Devotion to supreme skillful means
Abides in two distinct kinds:
That with an object is worldly;
That without an object is beyond the world.

Whoever teaches a single vehicle
Does not teach the Dharma well.
If one is not fully matured oneself,
How could one act for the sake of others?

If to beings wholly given to nonvirtue
One teaches the Dharma of the three vehicles,
That is delusion and bewilderment;
It does not belong to great awakening.

If to those eager to enter the three vehicles,
Who long for the true Dharma,
One teaches a Dharma that delights in saṃsāra,
That is not the nature of the wise.

Examining well with one-pointed mind,
One teaches according to wish and inclination.
Because this is supreme skillful means,
The wise praise it.

When beings who engage in evil deeds
Become vessels for the three vehicles,
One teaches Dharma suited to their faculties as vessels
And frees them from every evil."

Furthermore, son of good family, there is the bodhisattva great being's wheel of the great armor of great loving-kindness. A bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, eliminates the five kinds of sense pleasure; because he passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, he becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and will be worshiped, protected and served by all followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles.

What, then, is the wheel of the great armor of great loving-kindness? Son of good family, a bodhisattva's loving-kindness is of two kinds: loving-kindness focused on phenomena and loving-kindness focused on beings. Loving-kindness focused on phenomena is called the wheel of the great armor of great loving-kindness. Loving-kindness focused on beings is not called the great armor of great loving-kindness. Why is that? Loving-kindness focused on beings is shared with the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles. Śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas devote themselves to a patience focused on beings for the sake of their own benefit and happiness. Śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas do so for their own peace, their own nirvāṇa, the removal of their own obscurations, and the cutting off of their own afflictions; they do not devote themselves diligently, through loving-kindness focused on beings, for the sake of all beings. Therefore this loving-kindness is not called great loving-kindness, and it is not the great armor.

Loving-kindness focused on phenomena is not shared with followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles; only bodhisattva great beings are able to devote themselves to it. Bodhisattva great beings devote themselves to this loving-kindness focused on phenomena through the application of diligence, for the benefit and happiness of all beings. Bodhisattva great beings devote themselves to loving-kindness focused on phenomena with intensely striving diligence, in order to bring all beings fully to nirvāṇa and to cut off their afflictions completely. Therefore this loving-kindness is called the great armor of great loving-kindness.

Furthermore, a bodhisattva who devotes himself to loving-kindness focused on phenomena does not abide in the aggregates, does not abide in the sense sources, does not abide in the elements; he does not abide in the applications of mindfulness, and so on up to the limbs of the path of awakening. He does not abide in the desire realm, does not abide in the form realm, does not abide in the formless realm. He does not abide in this world, does not abide in the world beyond; he does not abide on the near shore, does not abide on the far shore; he does not abide in an object, and does not abide in the absence of an object. Thus, by devoting himself to loving-kindness focused on phenomena, a bodhisattva passes far beyond the grounds of the vehicles of all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. That is called the wheel of the great armor of a bodhisattva's great loving-kindness focused on phenomena.

Son of good family, a bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel of the great armor of great loving-kindness, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, eliminates the five kinds of sense pleasure, passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and will be worshiped, protected and served by all followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

"Śrāvakas and solitary victors,
Cultivating love focused on beings,
Carry thirteen faults in their minds
And strive for their own benefit.

Bodhisattvas of great renown,
For the sake of each and every being,
Cultivate the unshared great love
And abandon the thirteen faults of mind.

Having thoroughly abandoned the thirteen stains,
They direct themselves toward great awakening.
If one cultivates love focused on phenomena,
Becoming a field of merit is not far off.

If one dwells at ease in the thirteen powers,
One far surpasses all beings,
Just as the king of lions
Surpasses all other beasts.

Having utterly subdued the thirteen foes,
And abandoned clinging to the extremes of permanence and annihilation,
If the mind is stained by nothing whatsoever,
One swiftly attains great awakening."

Furthermore, son of good family, there is the bodhisattva great being's wheel of the great armor of great compassion. A bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, eliminates the five kinds of sense pleasure; because he passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, he becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and will be worshiped, protected and served by all followers of all the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles. How is that? All followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles practice compassion for the sake of their own benefit and happiness; they do not generate great compassion for the sake of the benefit and happiness of all beings.

Bodhisattva great beings do not generate great compassion for the sake of their own benefit and happiness alone; they generate great compassion for the sake of the benefit and happiness of all beings. Therefore, because a bodhisattva possesses the wheel of the great armor of great compassion, he passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and will be worshiped, protected and served by all followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles.

For the sake of the benefit and happiness of all beings, bodhisattva great beings bring beings to full maturity through the four means of attraction. Gathering all beings through generosity, out of great compassion and for the sake of their happiness, is as follows. They are without avarice in giving away jewels, wealth, riches, vehicles, servants, laborers, lands, kingdoms, sons, wives, and even their bodies and lives. Because they apply themselves to a practice without objectification, they do not see that there are beings to be trained; they do not see a giver, they do not see a recipient, they do not see things of wealth, they do not see the practice of giving, they do not see a fruit of having given, and so on, up to not seeing even nonobjectification. Such is the great compassion they generate.

For the sake of the benefit and happiness of all beings, with pleasant speech, with acting for their benefit, and with conduct consistent with that benefit, they teach and explain extensively through whatever means will train each one, up to not seeing even nonobjectification. Thus, at all times, with a supremely disciplined mind, a mind utterly at peace, an immeasurable mind, a mind that does not form anything regarding the aggregates, elements and sense sources, wherever they are born, bodhisattva great beings are unwavering; and with this wheel of the great armor of great compassion that does not abide, they never grow weary of bringing to full maturity all beings who have become vessels. Such is what is called a bodhisattva's wheel of the great armor of great compassion, and it is not shared with any śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas.

Son of good family, a bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel of the great armor of great compassion, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, eliminates the five sense pleasures, and so is called a bodhisattva great being. He passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and all followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles will worship, protect and serve him.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

"The very profound and sublime Dharma
Becomes the basis of great compassion.
It is utterly unfathomable, like space;
It has no abiding place and has no form.

The great diligence of the bodhisattva
Is endowed with great qualities;
Through the Victor's wisdom and great compassion
That hero thereby passes beyond the world.

Beings without protector or refuge,
Bound by the turbid suffering of saṃsāra,
Are washed by the water of great compassion,
And so those beings are set free.

By the compassion that bodhisattvas generate
The ocean of saṃsāra is dried up;
It is not done by the assemblies of the Śrāvaka Vehicle,
Nor by those who practice the Vehicle of Solitary Victors.

Beings of desire, anger and delusion,
Who are bound to fall into the lower realms,
Are washed by the water of great compassion
And set free from every suffering."

Furthermore, son of good family, there is the bodhisattva great being's wheel of the great armor of great and very steadfast patience, endowed with the analytical knowledge of language, equal to the expanse of space, immeasurable, endless and exceedingly vast, and with all the qualities of a buddha whatsoever, namely the samādhis and the dhāraṇīs. A bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel, from the first moment he generates the mind of awakening, eliminates the five kinds of sense pleasure, passes far beyond all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a great field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and all followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles will worship, protect and serve him.

What, then, is the bodhisattva great being's wheel of the great armor of great steadfastness, endowed with the analytical knowledge of language, equal to the expanse of space, immeasurable, endless and exceedingly vast, and with all the qualities of a buddha whatsoever, namely the samādhis and the dhāraṇīs? It is this. When bodhisattvas thoroughly examine all phenomena, then, just as the light of the moon's disk pervades the whole of space, their minds have become equanimous; in all the doors of samādhi that are signless, without support, without abiding and without stain, and in all the doors of dhāraṇī, there is no activity of mind. Free of the mind's clinging to the eye, to forms, to eye consciousness and to eye contact, and with no activity of mind, when the three kinds of inner feeling arise on the condition of eye contact, namely pleasant, painful, and neither pleasant nor painful, the mind is utterly at peace and without attachment toward those feelings.

In the same way, with the ear, sounds, ear consciousness and ear contact; the nose, odors, nose consciousness and nose contact; the tongue, tastes, tongue consciousness and tongue contact; the body, tangibles, body consciousness and body contact; and so on up to the mind, mental phenomena, mind consciousness and mind contact, the passage is to be expanded in the same way: all mind, mentation and consciousness is always at peace and without attachment. Toward the three kinds of feeling that arise from mind, mentation and consciousness, namely pleasant, painful, and neither pleasant nor painful, the mind is always at peace and without attachment.

Toward every aspect of the aggregates, elements and sense sources of the three times, there is no grasping and no activity of mind. Toward all the three realms, the three formations, the three tangibles, the three feelings, the three faculties, the three vehicles, the three vows and the three liberations, toward all such aspects, the mind abides in equality, utterly at peace, without abiding place, without signs and without grasping. Toward all generosity, moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration and wisdom, and all the perfections, it abides utterly at peace, with no activity of mind. In the same way, toward the four applications of mindfulness, the four correct exertions, the four bases of miraculous power, the five faculties, the five powers, the seven factors of awakening and the noble eightfold path, it likewise abides utterly at peace, with no activity of mind. Toward all the nine successive meditative attainments, too, it abides utterly at peace, with no activity of mind.

Furthermore, toward the three kinds of formative activity, the wisdom unobstructed regarding phenomena, the limbs of the path, and what leads to the nature of the path, there is likewise no grasping and no activity of mind. Toward every aspect of the all-base consciousness and what is not the all-base consciousness, of what is with appropriation and without appropriation, with defilement and without defilement, the near shore and the far shore, the small, the great and the immeasurable, doing and not doing, the virtuous, the nonvirtuous and the indeterminate, it abides utterly at peace, without grasping and with no activity of mind. Toward every aspect of all phenomena, from great loving-kindness, great compassion and the bringing of beings to full maturity through skillful means, up to the three things that need not be guarded, the four fearlessnesses and the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha, it abides utterly at peace, without grasping and with no activity of mind.

By the power of this wheel, bodhisattva great beings eliminate the faults of the three kinds of feeling, completely pacify all conceptual thought, and completely abandon all phenomena that are signs. Furthermore, it also brings forth the wheel of the great armor of skillful means in all the samādhis of the Eye of Space and of the Crown, and in the dhāraṇīs. Because a bodhisattva abides in that wheel, whatever remainder of sinful, nonvirtuous karma from times past would lead him to the lower realms and the Avīci hell, and all the karma of circling among the other states of existence and destinies, he eliminates entirely, without remainder, so that he does not experience its ripened fruit.

Furthermore, son of good family, it is like this. When black darkness covers the whole sky, the sun rises and dispels all the darkness. In the same way, a bodhisattva too, because he possesses the analytical knowledge of language that is equal to the expanse of space, immeasurable, endless, broad and vast, accomplishes and brings forth all the qualities of a buddha whatsoever, namely the samādhis and the dhāraṇīs, and also the wheel of the great armor of very steadfast patience. Furthermore, abiding in the wheel of the great armor of skillful means in the samādhis of the Eye of Space and of the Crown and in the dhāraṇīs, with the sun of immeasurable, spacelike wisdom he completely dispels the darkness of the four distortions. Whatever remainder of sinful, nonvirtuous karma from times past would lead him to the lower realms and the Avīci hell, and all the karma of circling in saṃsāra among the other states of existence and destinies, he dispels entirely, without remainder, and does not experience its ripened fruit.

Furthermore, by its power he gains mastery over the qualities of a buddha and becomes irreversible. He does not fall under the power of evil friends or follow them; he is never parted from seeing the buddhas, never parted from seeing bodhisattvas and śrāvaka attendants, never parted from hearing the Dharma, and never parted from making offerings to and serving the Saṅgha. His mind is never satisfied with any of the good qualities, and so on without interruption until awakening; he is never parted from the recollection of the Buddha, and at the very least it continues without interruption even in his dreams.

Son of good family, how does a bodhisattva great being, because he possesses the analytical knowledge of language that is equal to the expanse of space, immeasurable, endless, broad and vast, bring forth all the qualities of a buddha whatsoever, namely the samādhis and the dhāraṇīs, and the wheel of the great armor of very steadfast patience? It is this. When bodhisattvas abide in this samādhi of entering the absorptions from the first meditative concentration up to the fourth, entering the absorptions from the sphere of infinite space up to the sphere of neither perception nor nonperception, and entering the samādhi of the cessation of perception and feeling, they cut off the three kinds of feeling and the three formations; without formative activity and without wavering activity of mind, they cut off feeling, perception, volition, awareness, attention and all the rest. Abiding in this samādhi, they partake of the food of meditative concentration for one day, or for up to seven days. Even when they rise from that samādhi, the mind is utterly at peace and without grasping, and they abide in calm stillness.

Furthermore, they settle evenly in the samādhi of emptiness that reaches the limit of the ultimate. When they abide in that samādhi, because the mind is utterly even, it is without attachment like space, and from every pore of the body drops of sweet dew like stars come forth, dispelling all the torment bound up with the afflictions. When they rise from that samādhi, they attain right mindfulness, and the body is pervaded by a special joy. Just as a son of the gods and Maheśvara (Śiva), entering all the samādhis of bliss, experience utter bliss in every pore of the body, so that bodhisattva, his body touched by bliss, comes to recollect the Buddha. Through recollecting the Buddha he sees the Buddha, and sees no other sign than that.

At that time, if that bodhisattva brings a single buddha to mind, he sees a single buddha. If he brings many buddhas to mind, he sees many buddhas. If he brings the Buddha's body to mind as small, he sees the Buddha's body as small. If he brings the Buddha's body to mind as large, he sees the Buddha's body as large. If he brings the Buddha's body to mind as immeasurable, he sees the Buddha's body as immeasurable. If he brings to mind his own body as possessing the marks of the Buddha's body, he sees that body of his own as perfect in the marks, just like the Buddha's body. If he brings to mind the bodies of others as the Buddha's body, he sees the bodies of others as perfect in the marks, just like the Buddha's body. If he brings to mind sentient beings, things that are not sentient beings, and everything whatsoever that belongs to form as the Buddha's body possessing the marks, he sees sentient beings, things that are not sentient beings, and everything whatsoever that belongs to form as perfect in the marks, just like the Buddha's body, and he does not see form in any other way.

At that time the bodhisattva further thinks: "All phenomena and all forms are like an illusion. Since they are not real, I shall completely cut off all phenomena without remainder, such as the three kinds of feeling and the three formations." Thinking this, he enters into the samādhi of cessation. Abiding in that samādhi, he cuts off everything completely, just as he had resolved beforehand, and lives on the food of the taste of samādhi. He remains for as long as he is able, whether seven days, or two weeks of seven days, or three, or four, or five, or six, or seven, or eight, or even for immeasurable hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of eons, eating the food of samādhi. When he rises from that samādhi, his mind is utterly at peace, he is attached to nothing, and he dwells in bliss. If he enters the samādhi that reaches the limit of the ultimate, then, just as explained before, he again comprehends everything from bringing to mind the Buddha's body possessing the marks up to seeing that all phenomena and all forms are like an illusion and unreal.

Son of noble family, this is called the wheel of the great armor of the bodhisattva great being's analytical knowledge of language (niruktipratisaṃvid), which is equal to the expanse of space, immeasurable, limitless, vast and extensive, and is all the qualities of a buddha whatsoever: all the samādhis, all the dhāraṇīs, and utterly firm patience. Bodhisattva great beings who possess this wheel are supremely skilled in means with regard to the Eye of Space, the samādhis of the Crown, and the dhāraṇīs; they abandon everything without remainder and do not take on the fruit of karmic maturation.

Furthermore, son of noble family, it is like this. When destruction by fire is about to occur in a world-system, five suns rise all at once, and the waters of the ponds, great ponds, rivers, great rivers, lakes and great lakes in the world-system all dry up without remainder. In the same way, the bodhisattva too, possessing the analytical knowledge of language that is equal to the expanse of space, immeasurable, limitless, vast and extensive, brings forth all the qualities of a buddha: the samādhis, the dhāraṇīs, and the wheel of the great armor of utterly firm patience. Furthermore, he abides in and brings forth the wheel of the great armor of skill in means with regard to the Eye of Space, the samādhis of the Crown, and the dhāraṇīs. All the karma, driven by the residue of the sinful, unwholesome actions of past times, by which one circles in saṃsāra in the lower realms such as Avīci and in other states of existence and destinies, he dries up completely without remainder, and he does not take on the fruit of karmic maturation.

Furthermore, son of noble family, it is like this. When destruction by water occurs in the world, in the great trichiliocosm world-system the four great continents of this small world, the eight subcontinents, Meru, king of mountains, and all the lesser mountains are washed away by the water and are destroyed and consumed without remainder. In the same way, the bodhisattva too, possessing the analytical knowledge of language that is equal to the expanse of space, immeasurable, limitless, vast and extensive, accomplishes and brings forth all the qualities of a buddha whatsoever: the samādhis, the dhāraṇīs, and the wheel of the great armor of utterly firm patience. Because he abides in and brings forth the wheel of the great armor of skill in means with regard to the Eye of Space, the samādhis of the Crown, and the dhāraṇīs, he also abides in the wheel of the great armor of skill with regard to the karma, driven by the residue of the sinful, unwholesome actions of past times, by which one circles in saṃsāra in the lower realms such as Avīci and in other states of existence and destinies.

By abiding in this wheel, the sun of immeasurable, space-like wisdom arises, and he completely dispels the four inversions in his own body and all the darkness of ignorance. All the karma, driven by the residue of the sinful, unwholesome actions of past times, by which one circles in saṃsāra in the lower realms such as Avīci and in other states of existence and destinies, he also dispels completely without remainder, and he does not experience the fruit of karmic maturation either.

Son of noble family, a bodhisattva great being who possesses this wheel, from the first generation of the mind of awakening onward, eliminates the five kinds of desire, utterly transcends all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, becomes a field of merit for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and is honored, protected and served by all followers of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles.

Through the power of this wheel, he gains mastery over all the qualities of a buddha, becomes irreversible, and does not fall under the power of evil friends. He is never parted from seeing all the buddhas, never parted from seeing the bodhisattvas and the śrāvaka attendants, never parted from hearing the Dharma, and never parted from making offerings to the Saṅgha and serving it. His mind is never sated with any good qualities, and this continues unbroken until his full awakening. As for never being parted from the constant recollection of the Buddha, he is not parted from it even in the slightest degree, not even in a dream. In this way that bodhisattva swiftly completes the accumulations of merit and wisdom, and before long he fully awakens to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening in a pure buddhafield. In that buddhafield all sentient beings are born miraculously; their marks and forms resemble those of the buddhas, the blessed ones; their afflictions are thin and slight; and every one of them dwells happily in the Great Vehicle.

Then, in order to restate these points clearly, the Blessed One spoke these verses:

Those who wish to become vessels for the Dharma
Should completely cut off all the afflictions.
If one is absorbed in genuine emptiness,
There is nothing at all that is hard to do.

In order to cut all the bonds of existence,
Strive earnestly to enter meditative absorption.
Because one possesses the qualities of samādhi,
One will without doubt gain wisdom hard to fathom.

If one thoroughly cultivates the formless dhyānas
And examines the samādhi of cessation and emptiness,
The supreme understanding that recollects the Buddha arises,
And all one's misdeeds will be completely exhausted.

All phenomena, existent and nonexistent,
Are overcome by examining them as genuine emptiness.
Having completely cleared away the courses to the lower realms,
One will always come to see many buddhas.

Having thoroughly cultivated genuine emptiness,
One should strive in all other wholesome qualities.
If one makes offerings to each and every buddha,
One will swiftly attain the fruit of buddhahood.

If one acts as a spiritual friend to all sentient beings
And clears away the sickness of the afflictions,
One will swiftly be born in a pure buddhafield,
Fully awaken, and attain great awakening.

Sentient beings who possess the marks of a buddha
Will completely fill that buddhafield.
All of them will aspire to the vehicle of the buddhas,
And will entirely abandon the vehicles of the śrāvakas and solitary victors.

This concludes the seventh chapter, called "The Characteristics of a Field of Merit," from the noble, extensive great sūtra, The Ten Wheels of the Bodhisattva Kṣitigarbha.

When the Blessed One taught this discourse of the Great Vehicle, there were in that assembly bodhisattva great beings, as numerous as the grains of sand of the river Ganges, who in times past had trained in the recollection of the Buddha. Now, having heard the Blessed One explain the practice of applying oneself earnestly to the recollection of the Buddha, they attained every gateway to the samādhi of recollecting the Buddha.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard this Dharma, attained every gateway of the dhāraṇī called "The Flower Garland That Lives on Samādhi."

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, attained every gateway of the dhāraṇī called "The Heroic March That Relies on the Light of Lightning."

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, attained every kind of conforming patience, the patience that turns toward the Dharma and rests upon its shining light.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, became free of dust and stain; the pure Dharma eye regarding phenomena arose in them, and they attained the fruit of stream-entry.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, attained the fruit of once-returning.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, attained the fruit of non-returning.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, attained the supreme fruit of arhatship.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, set about escaping from the prison of the three realms; relying on the Blessed One, they went forth and entered the true Dharma.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, dwelling happily in the ten paths of wholesome action for as long as they lived, generated the resolve for the Śrāvaka Vehicle and became irreversible.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, dwelt happily in the ten paths of wholesome action for as long as they lived, and became irreversible from the resolve they had generated for the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, dwelt happily in the ten paths of wholesome action for as long as they lived, generated the resolve for the Great Vehicle, and became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, attained the worldly right view. Through the power of that right view, the causes of going to the lower realms, the karma of thorough affliction, and all the afflictions were purified, and the causes of going to the higher realms, genuine aspirations, and all wholesome actions arose in them and increased.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, went for refuge to the Three Jewels and took the vows of a layman. They became eager to make offerings to the Buddha, delighted in hearing the Dharma, and devoted to serving the Saṅgha, and their resolve to exert themselves diligently day and night became boundless.

Furthermore, immeasurable, countless sentient beings, having heard the Blessed One's teaching, completely abandoned all that inclines toward wrong ways, all that leans toward wrong ways, all bad understanding and all bad actions. Gaining unshakable faith in the Dharma of the Buddha, they completely abandoned the household life and went forth in purity.

Then the Blessed One spoke these words to the bodhisattva great being Ākāśagarbha:

"Son of noble family, I entrust into your hands this gateway of the Dharma, the great exposition called The Ten Wheels of the Bodhisattva Kṣitigarbha. Retain it, and spread it far and wide. Whatever sentient beings read this Dharma discourse, recite it, bring it to mind, explain it at length to others, or abide in genuine conduct, you must protect them by means of these ten things and enable them to attain benefit and happiness for a long time. What are the ten? They are as follows. Guarding their means of livelihood and all the wealth they possess, so that it does not decline; guarding them from all harm, so that they are free of harm; guarding them from wrong views and wrong leanings, so that they abandon the ten paths of unwholesome action; guarding them from all harm to their body and speech, so that there is none; guarding them from all blame and slander, so that there is none; guarding them so that none of their moral discipline and conduct declines; guarding them so that all nonhuman beings, disturbances of the great elements, untimely illnesses, and everything unwholesome are cleared away from them; guarding them so that no untimely or improper happenings, ill omens, or epidemics befall them; guarding them so that even when the time of death comes they see all the buddhas; and fully guarding them so that, after passing from that life, they are born in the happy realms and attain benefit and happiness. Son of noble family, whatever sentient beings read this Dharma discourse, recite it, bring its meaning to mind, explain it at length to others, or abide in genuine conduct, you must thoroughly protect them by means of these ten things, bless them, and through these ten things bring them benefit and happiness for a long time."

Then the bodhisattva great being Ākāśagarbha said this to the Blessed One: "So it is. Blessed One, I will retain this Dharma discourse and spread it far and wide. Whatever sentient beings read this Dharma discourse, recite it, bring its meaning to mind, teach it at length to others, and abide in genuine conduct, I will thoroughly protect them by means of these ten things and bring them benefit and happiness for a long time."

When the Blessed One had spoken this sūtra, the bodhisattva great being Ākāśagarbha, the bodhisattva great being Kṣitigarbha, the bodhisattva great being Vajragarbha, and the bodhisattva great being Questioner of Doubts by Reasoning, who were present in that assembly, together with the great Brahmā Devagarbha and the whole great assembly of gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, humans and nonhumans, rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said.

This concludes the eighth chapter, called "The Entrustment for Attaining Happiness," from The Ten Wheels of the Bodhisattva Kṣitigarbha in the Great Collection.

This completes the Great Vehicle sūtra from the Great Collection called The Ten Wheels of the Bodhisattva Kṣitigarbha.

It was translated, edited and finalized by the Chinese preceptor Hwashang Zabmo and others, together with the chief editor-translator, the monk Nampar Mitok.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

The mountain's name is given as the Tibetan transcribes it (Khaladeya); its Sanskrit form is uncertain (the Chinese version suggests Khalatiya). "The teaching called Candragarbha" refers to the preceding section of the Great Collection (Mahāsaṃnipāta), the Candragarbha Sūtra, here named as a text rather than as the bodhisattva.  The epithet of Śakra here, "Born Stainless," is translated from the Tibetan; its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. "Eighty thousand koṭis of niyutas" renders the stock vast number given in the Tibetan.  "His form, deep and vast" follows the Tibetan as written (gzugs); a reading gzungs, "his dhāraṇī," would also fit the simile, but the text has been kept as it stands.  The name of the bodhisattva rendered "Inquiring Rightly into Doubts" is translated from its Tibetan meaning; its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. In the verse line about sentient beings coming before the Victor, the Tibetan has "true" (bden pa), which makes little sense; the evident reading "happy" (bde ba) has been followed.  The names of the absorptions are translated from the Tibetan; their Sanskrit forms are not attested with confidence here.  The names of the absorptions are rendered literally from the Tibetan; their Sanskrit forms are not attested with confidence. "Wisdom Free of Conflict" likely reflects araṇā-jñāna. The text shifts from present to future time ("in times to come") while keeping present-tense verbs; this is rendered as it stands.  "Feeble" reads ཉམ་ཆུང་བདག་གིས as an evident slip for ཉམ་ཆུང་བ་དག་གིས ("those who are feeble"), which matches the parallel clauses. The phrase མི་འཕོ་བར་གྱུར་པ ("do not shift") in the list of afflictions is taken as being unable to be rid of them; its exact sense is uncertain.  སྲུལ་པོ, ལུས་སྲུལ་པོ and མདངས་ཟ་བ are taken as the standard renderings of pūtana, kaṭapūtana, and ojohāra ("vitality-eaters"). In the list of worldly wishes, ཐེག་པ is read as a conveyance, though it could also mean a spiritual vehicle.  ར་བ་མཚེ་མ is taken as this text's name for the Yāma heaven, since it stands in the place of Yāma between Tuṣita and the Thirty-Three. གནོན་པོ ("oppressor") is a class of spirit I cannot identify with confidence, so it is rendered literally. སྤོས་ཀྱི་བ་ལང ("fragrant ox") is taken as the gandhahastin, the fragrant elephant, which the usual parallels list here.  The mountain's name, transcribed in Tibetan as Khalateya, is given as the Tibetan spells it; it is the setting of this sūtra, known elsewhere as Khalatika or Khadiraka. In the second summary the text reads "focus" (དམིགས་པ) where the first list had "mindfulness" (དྲན་པ); it is translated as written.  Of the six goddesses named at the start, only Śrī (dpal ldan lha mo) and Dṛḍhā (brtan pa, the earth goddess) are identified with confidence; the others (bkra shis chen mo, mchod dbyangs, chu can, 'od chen) are translated by meaning as Great Auspiciousness, Song of Worship, Watery One and Great Light. The verse section introduces Śakra's praise but its close falls outside this piece, so the opening quotation mark of his speech is left open.  "The thirteen many conditions" and "the thirteen conditions" in the opening verses are rendered as written; the text does not enumerate them here. "What is like a vajra" in Kṣitigarbha's question presumably refers to the vajra-like samādhi that severs the last afflictions.  The final paragraph of the simile continues into the next piece, so the closing quotation mark is not yet given.  Where the bodhisattvas' speech ends is not marked in the Tibetan; the description that follows (with honorific verbs) parallels the enthronement of the prince in the preceding simile and is rendered in the Buddha's first person, since he is the speaker and the one so enthroned. "Hard as a vajra" reads sra bdag as sra ba dag.  The list of attributes of the five aggregates of clinging is terse; "they are not without accumulation" renders སྤུངས་པ་མེད་པ་མ་ཡིན་པ literally, a double negative that sits oddly among the other negations and may reflect a difficulty in the source or its translation. In "the cause of existence, the field, and the moisture that envelops it", the imagery is of karma as field and craving as moisture.  "By something like the spreading of grass" renders རྩ་དགྲམ་པ (read as རྩྭ་དགྲམ་པ), evidently the Vinaya procedure of "covering over with grass" (tṛṇaprastāraka) for settling disputes. "Brief separation and bringing them to shame" renders the terse སྐད་ཅིག་དགར་ཞིང་སྨ་དབབ་པ, apparently temporary exclusion and public censure. In "rebukes and scolds", བརྒྱད་པ is read as རྒྱད་པ "to revile".  "Mynah birds" renders བྱ་ཤ་ལ, taken as a transcription of Skt. śārikā, often paired with parrots; the identification is not certain. The phrase ཞེས་གསུངས་སོ ("So he spoke") closes the Buddha's account of the fourth wheel within the narrative and is kept as written. "Let them delight freely" renders ཅི་ད་གར་རྩེར་བཅུག.  The first sentence switches from "I" to "he" as in the Tibetan, where the Buddha speaks of the Tathāgata in the third person and of himself in the first. "And so on as in the passage before" renders གཞུང་དེ་བཞིན་དུ་རྒྱས་པར, an abbreviation that refers back to the fuller description of the fifth royal wheel.  "The counting of the breath" renders དབུགས་འགྲང་བ, the counting practice of breath meditation described earlier in the text.  The "ninth wheel" is the ninth of the ten wheels after which the sūtra is named; in the closing paragraph the Tibetan adds a plural marker (literally "these ninth wheels of a buddha"), but the sense, matching the king's "this ninth wheel" above, is the single ninth wheel. Attachment and aversion in the list of the ten non-virtues stand for covetousness and malice.  After the name Subhadra the Tibetan has the syllables "rab de" before "and others"; they do not yield a clear name, and I have read them as part of the closing "and others besides" rather than invent a further disciple. The western continent is spelled "ban glang spyod" for the usual "ba glang spyod" (Godānīya), an evident copyist's slip. The king's "tenth wheel" is literally plural ("these tenth wheels"), but the sense is the single tenth of the ten wheels.  "This great earth, a hundred thousand of them" renders the unusual "brgya stong sa chen po"; it probably refers to the great earths of the vast buddha field described just before. The name of the teaching, literally "the great instruction in knowledge, abiding, and what is not to be practiced," corresponds to the next chapter's title, "abiding and conduct"; I have kept the negative as written.  The phrase rendered earlier as "on what to abide in and what is not to be practiced" is shown by this passage to mean things "not to be abided in or practiced," the negative governing both verbs; the teaching's title has been kept as before for continuity, and the lists here are rendered by the clearer sense. Kudaka, Arādaka and Deśukupālaki are given as the Tibetan spells them; the first two may be the meditation teachers Udraka and Ārāḍa, who in Buddhist legend fell away from their absorptions, but this is not certain, and the third I cannot identify.  Two words are uncertain. In Devagarbha's list of punishments, "brgyad" is rendered "mocked" tentatively; it does not give a clear sense as written. The simile for the corrupt monk, "srin kwa lwa," is obscure; "a worm-eaten husk filled with filth" follows the likely sense (srin, "worm") and the familiar image of a monk "rotten within."  སྲིན་ཀྭ་ལྭ is obscure; it is rendered as "a worm-ridden thing" on the reading སྲིན (worm, vermin), and the exact object of comparison is uncertain. The fruit called བརྟན ("Steadfast") could not be identified with confidence and is translated literally. ཚུལ་དུ་འཇུག་པ and ཚུལ་ནས་བྱུང་བ are taken as entering the rains retreat and its concluding ceremony (pravāraṇā). The closing མཚམས་མེད་པ here refers to the deeds of immediate retribution (ānantarya), not the hell.  In "among acts of taking what is not given," the text reads ན་ང་ན, taken as a copyist's slip for ནང་ན ("among"). In "follows worldly gods," the text has རྗེད ("forget"), which makes little sense here; it is read as རྗེས ("follow"). Here མཚམས་མེད་པ refers to the deeds of immediate retribution (ānantarya), not the hell.  The recurring phrase མི་གནས་པ་དང་མི་བྱ་བ is rendered "that exclude one from communion and must not be done," taking མི་གནས་པ as "not dwelling together" with the Saṅgha, the consequence of a pārājika offense. The distinction between evils by prohibition (prajñapti-avadya) and evils by nature (prakṛti-avadya) is the standard Vinaya one.  Upāli asks how the Saṅgha should act "at a time when [the Buddha] is no longer present" (མ་མཆིས་པའི་དུས་ན), taken to refer to the period after the Buddha's passing. ཕྱོགས་བཞིའི་དགེ་འདུན་གྱི་རུང་བར་བྱེད་པ is taken as the monastery attendants (kalpikāra) who make things allowable for the Saṅgha.  Here མ་མོ refers to the mātṛkā, the doctrinal matrices of the Abhidharma, held alongside the sūtras and Vinaya; it is not the class of goddesses. "The seven methods of settling disputes" are the adhikaraṇa-śamatha of the Vinaya. The weed in the barley field (ནས་ལ་གནོད་པ, literally "what harms the barley") is not named.  "They have only a semblance of moral discipline" renders ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས་འདྲ་ལ་ཞིང, whose wording is awkward; it may be a slip for ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས་འཆལ་ཞིང ("they have broken their moral discipline"). The phrase "behave like dogs at the sound of the conch" is literal; the conch summoned monks to meals, so it likely pictures greedy monks running to food like dogs. The term rendered "caṇḍāla" (གདོལ་པ་ཅན) describes people who act like outcastes, not their actual caste.  The forest-dwelling monks are described with བས་མཐར་གནས་པ, "dwelling in the remote borderlands," i.e. hermits of the wilderness. Three readings follow evident copyist slips: བ་དག་ནི་སྟོད is read as བདག་ནི་སྟོད, "they praise themselves"; དཀྲུགས་མ་བྱེད is read as "stir up discord", since the negative makes no sense in a list of accusations; and ཁེ་དང་གྲགས་པ་ལ་སྐྱོ, literally "weary of profit and fame", is read as craving them, as the following "never satisfied" requires. In the list of protectors, ལྷ appears twice ("gods ... and deities"); the second may stand for another class of spirits.  The phrase rendered "and so on, in full as in the passage above, until" (ནས་གཞུང་དེ་བཞིན་དུ་རྒྱས་པར) is the sūtra's own abbreviation, telling the reader to supply the earlier passage in full. ཁྱིམ་པའི་བུས་པ is read as ཁྱིམ་པ་སྐྱེས་པ, "householder men", since it is paired with "women". མིག་འཛར་བ is taken as "put out the eyes".  "Something of the pure Dharma still remains with him, if only in name" renders a phrase that literally reads "there is merely the speech of the pure Dharma"; the sense seems to be that some trace of his ordained status remains. The elephant's name ཨུ་པ་ལིའི་མིག is rendered "Upāli's Eye", following the name list; the element ཨུ་པ་ལི may instead stand for utpala (blue lotus), giving "Lotus Eye".  Several names here could not be identified with confidence, so their meanings are given in English: the king དགྲ་ལས་རྒྱལ ("Victor over Foes", perhaps Skt. Śatrujit), and the rākṣasīs མཚོན་ཆའི་མིག ("Weapon Eye") and བོང་བུ་སོ ("Donkey Tooth"). The name of the charnel ground, ཁ་ལམ་པ, has no clear meaning and is given in phonetics as "Khalampa". The verse Weapon Eye's retinue speaks has five lines in the Tibetan.  The names of the two rākṣasīs (ཁྲོ་གཉེར་འཕྲལ་སྐྲ་ཅན, reading འཕྲལ as དཔྲལ "forehead", and མཚོན་ཆའི་གདོང) and of King བསོད་ནམས་ཆེན་པོ could not be identified with Sanskrit originals with confidence, so their meanings are translated, as with Donkey Tooth earlier. "Caṇḍāla kings" renders རྒྱལ་རིགས་གདོལ་པ་ཅན, kṣatriyas who behave like outcastes.  བལ་གླང་རྨིས་པ is taken as "elephant in rut" (musth). The "three wheels of evil" evidently refers to a set defined earlier in the sūtra. The phrase "the fruit of reaching the final state" renders མཐའི་གནས་བྱས་པའི་འབྲས་བུ, apparently the final fruit of arhatship. In the first list, ཞེན་པ is read as ཞན་པ "weak", as in the parallel later in the passage.  The clause སྐལ་བ་མེད་པ་མེད་དོ reads literally "there is no lack of fortune," which contradicts the sense of the passage; it is translated by the evident meaning, "they are utterly without the fortune for it," the second negative apparently being a scribal slip or emphatic. "Vows of evil" renders སྡིག་པའི་སྡོམ་པ, the commitments to harmful livelihoods (Skt. asaṃvara or pāpa-saṃvara).  "The fifth fascicle" renders the division marker བམ་པོ་ལྔ་པ in the Tibetan text. Kṣitigarbha's question continues into the next piece, where the main clause (how much merit such virtuous rulers and others obtain) is given.  The opening comparison is syntactically compressed: the instrumental "by the merit of untying the bonds of the arhats" is read as the standard of comparison, so that the offerings and stūpa-building just mentioned fall far short of the merit of freeing and serving the arhats described in the preceding passage; the final series renders the stock formula of incomparability (saṃkhyā, gaṇanā, upamā, upaniṣad). Khrag khrig is taken as niyuta, consistent with the house glossary. "Does not make the Dharma of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha Vehicles his own realization" renders mngon du mi byed, meaning he does not adopt their goal as his own, while still not disparaging them.
