"Whose resolve to protect the land never turns back" renders sems phyir mi ldog pa, describing the dhāraṇīs offered; the dhāraṇī's own name, the Irreversible Wheel, echoes this. The dhāraṇī text itself presumably follows in the next piece. In the first list of ten, the first item, long life, is phrased without the "arising and increase" formula that the other nine carry, and this is kept as written.  The dhāraṇī is given syllable for syllable as the Tibetan spells it. In the verses, mtshams med sems can dmyal ba is taken as the Avīci hell, the hell of uninterrupted torment. The line mkhas pa ma yin yang dag pa dag is obscure; it is read as "the unwise, and even the pure," taking yang as "even"; an alternative is "the unwise and the untrue." The question closing Devagarbha's verse (gang dag the tsom sel cing mi ldog) is kept as a question, as gang dag suggests; its sense is compressed. "Thereby" in "they will thereby become an ocean" reads des as written, though it may be a slip for nges, "surely," as in the parallel line above. The final verse continues into the next piece.  The opening lines complete a verse begun in the previous piece. In Vajragarbha's verses, the lines in the first person ("this pure assembly of mine", "I have not permitted") are read as his quotation of the Buddha's earlier words, so they are set within the quotation; "like the ocean that keeps no corpse" renders "like the taste of the ocean," evoking the stock image of the ocean that does not tolerate a corpse, which is uncertain. The line "Still, make offerings to them as to me" interprets the compressed phyir yang nga la mchod pa byos. The couplet beginning "Loving and compassionate one" is elliptical and its construction is uncertain. "The ten liberations through the Śrāvaka Vehicle" renders rnam par grol ba bcu po literally; the ten may instead refer to ten kinds of persons. "Higher conduct" (lhag pa'i spyod pa) and "conviction" (mos pa) render the Tibetan literally.  "Resort to worldly gods" renders lha rjed pa; rjed ("forget") does not fit, and the sense of worshipping or propitiating worldly gods is taken from context, possibly reading a similar verb such as mchod. The ten things not to be dwelt in are completed here by dividing the long sentence about seeking wealth and about illness into the ninth and tenth items, which the parallel with the following explanations does not fully confirm. "A śramaṇa who corrupts the path" renders lam sun 'byin pa'i dge sbyong (mārgadūṣin), the last of the four classical types of śramaṇa. In "is not a vessel of the Dharma of the noble ones; yet the Tathāgata," the particle nam is read as a connective, and in "destroying the eye of the true Dharma" the verb bcar is read in the sense of ruining or gouging out.  གསོ་སྦྱིན is read as གསོ་སྦྱོང (poṣadha), the evident reading in a list of formal monastic acts. ཚུལ་གྱི་ལས is rendered "procedural acts"; it may stand for a specific rite such as the pravāraṇā. The two similes for the shameless monk (སྲིན་ཀྭ་ལྭ, some shellfish or snail, "rotten within and full of filth"; and "the conduct of a dog at the sound of a conch") are rendered literally; their exact sense is uncertain. "The three supreme and sacred kinds of action" is not further specified in the text. ཐ་མ for the first-listed path-teaching śramaṇa is taken as "lowest" in the graded list of five.  The list "there are no virtuous kṣatriyas, no śramaṇas ..." follows the Tibetan word order, in which "virtuous" (དགེ་བ) qualifies kṣatriyas; the sense is a nihilistic denial of the social classes and their merit. ཚོང་དཔོན may also be rendered "guild masters".  The four kinds of śramaṇa here correspond to the well-known Indian classification: the victor of the path (mārgajina), the teacher of the path (mārgadeśaka), one who lives by the path (mārgajīvin), and one who corrupts the path (mārgadūṣin). "The sixth fascicle" renders the bam po division marker in the Tibetan. In the corpse simile, the clause beginning འབགས་ན is taken as "whoever is smeared with it reeks of it."  བདག་གཏི་མུག is taken as "delusion about the self" (ātmamoha), the root of the pride mentioned next. In the passage on those who have heard only the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, the Tibetan reads "the unsurpassed vehicle" without "great," and this is kept.  ཁ་ན་མ་ཐོ་བ is rendered "wrong" (Skt. avadya, a reprehensible act) and དམ་ཚིག "sacred commitment." The closing first-person sentences, opened by "in the past" (སྔར), are taken as the Buddha recounting his own former conduct, which continues in the next piece.  In the verse on formal acts, ཞུ་བའི་ལས is taken as the Saṅgha's formal acts carried out by motion. The verse of the merchants alludes to the well-known tale of merchants rescued from an island of rākṣasīs by clinging to a wondrous horse. In this piece བསོད་ནམས་ཆེན་པོ ("great merit") and བྱམས་པ ("loving-kindness") are ordinary words, not the names Great Merit and Maitreya. The final quoted verses continue into the next piece, so the closing quotation mark is left open.  The verse line སློབ་དཔོན་འཆབ, literally "conceal the master," is taken as "relying on a master," since "conceal" makes no sense here; this reading is uncertain. ཀུན་ནས་ལྡང་བའི་ཆོས is understood as the active afflictions (Skt. paryavasthāna). The second list of ten as written yields nine clearly separate items, and is translated as it stands. The definitions of "abiding" and "practice" at the end are terse and are rendered literally.  In "little diligence" the text reads བརྩོན་འགྲུས་ཆུང་བདག་ལ; this is taken as a slip for ཆུང་བ་དག་ལ ("to those of little diligence"), as the parallel sentence that follows confirms. The phrase "come down to awakening" renders བྱང་ཆུབ་ལ་འབབ་པ literally (flowing or descending into awakening). The sentence comparing hell with the annihilationist view is terse in the Tibetan; it is rendered as stressing that the annihilationist view among humans is worse than hellish suffering.  "Proclaims annihilation and cessation" renders ཆད་པ་དང་འགོག་པར་སྨྲ་བ, here in the pejorative sense of a nihilist teaching that things are cut off and cease. In ཆད་པས་གཅོད the same word ཆད་པ means "punishment", so it is rendered "punishes". The phrase "from not having given up killing up to wrong view" refers in brief to the ten nonvirtuous actions.  The opening sentences repeat, almost word for word, a passage that appears earlier in the source; they are translated again because they stand again in the Tibetan here. "Practice without appropriation" renders མི་ལེན་པར་སྤྱོད་པ, practice that does not take up or grasp anything. The "three wheels of complete victory" correspond to the three miracles of a buddha: miraculous power, revealing what is in others' minds, and instruction. འཇིག་རྟེན་རྒྱང་པ་ན་པ is taken as a variant spelling of the term for the Lokāyata materialists (རྒྱང་འཕེན་པ). The repeated formula "X itself is not empty; what is other than X is not empty" is rendered literally as it stands.  "The three things to be guarded" renders བསྲུང་བར་བྱ་བ་གསུམ་པོ as it stands; in lists of a buddha's qualities this item usually appears as the three things that need no guarding (arakṣya), which may be what is meant here.  "Fascicle seven" renders the section marker བམ་པོ་བདུན་པ, which marks the start of the seventh fascicle of the Tibetan text. "Has burned the seed of all gods and humans" means he has destroyed the karmic seed for rebirth as a god or a human.  "Restate" renders བཟླས་པ here in its ordinary sense of repeating or recapitulating, not mantra recitation. "Ascetic practices" renders སྦྱངས་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན (dhūtaguṇa). The line "One becomes a vessel of the supreme vehicle" closes the pratyekabuddha stanza as the Tibetan stands; its referent is somewhat ambiguous. The assembly's speech continues into the next piece.  The four stages named here (lesser, middling and highest forbearance, and the supreme worldly quality) follow the text's own list. The name of the absorption རྒྱལ་མཚན་ལ་དམིགས་པ is translated literally as "Focused on the Victory Banner", since a Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. The assembly's speech continues into the next piece.  "Some of us spoke like this" renders ལ་ལ་ནི་འདི་སྐད་དུ, which loosely introduces the list of offenses that follows. The speech continues into the next piece.  མཚམས་མེད་པ here refers to the deeds of immediate retribution (ānantarya), the five gravest crimes, not to the hell of that name used earlier; it is translated accordingly. The phrase སྡིག་པའི་གནན is read as a comparison ("compared with the evil of ..."), which the sense of the following sentences supports.  སྣང་མཛད, the last of the thousand buddhas of the Fortunate Eon, is rendered Rocana, the name usually given to that buddha. In the phrase སོ་སོར་ཤོགས་ཤིག, ཤོགས is read as a slip for བཤགས ("confess"), which the context requires; likewise ཡོན་ཏན་བཅས is read as ཡོན་ཏན་བཅབས ("conceal their good qualities"), as in the parallel lists earlier. The sentence beginning "it would be easier" reads the final negation as a comparison: dwelling in that hell is preferable to the evil rebirth described.  "Forbearance regarding the Dharma" renders ཆོས་ཀྱི་བཟོད་པ (Skt. dharmakṣānti), the acceptance of the nature of phenomena attained on the path. མ་མཆིས་པའི་དུས, literally "the time that is not," is taken as the future (times not yet come), matching the parallel resolve "never to do them again."  The sixth and seventh qualities are worded identically in this Tibetan ("do not dwell in this world"); they are translated as they stand. In Vajragarbha's second question, མི་སྐུར་བར is read as "do not encourage" (as if བསྐུལ), matching the Buddha's parallel "do not establish others in training"; read literally it would be "without disparaging." "The ten courses of wholesome action" renders the daśakuśalakarmapatha.  "Free of error" renders འཁྲུལ་པ་མེད་པ, literally "without confusion or mistake," i.e. not misjudging or wrongly treating each vehicle and its practitioners. "The eighth fascicle" marks the start of the eighth bampo (division of the Tibetan text).  "The four currents" renders རྒྱུ་བ་བཞི, literally "four flowings"; it most likely denotes the four floods (ogha) or the four outflows (āsrava), but the text does not specify. "The four worldly distortions" are the four inverted views (viparyāsa): taking the impermanent as permanent, suffering as happiness, the impure as pure, and what is without self as a self. In the opening list this piece reads "pratyekabuddha attendants" where Vajragarbha's question had "śrāvaka attendants"; it is translated as it stands.  "The obscurations of life" renders ཚེའི་སྒྲིབ་པ, an obscuration bearing on lifespan, which fits the context of killing; the text does not define it further.  "Make no mistake regarding" renders འཁྲུལ་པ་མེད་པ, that is, he will not err in his attitude toward these vehicles and their followers (and so will not disparage them). "The five destinies" renders རྒྱུད་ལྔ, the five realms of rebirth. "Shared with the five dangers" refers to the traditional five threats to wealth, such as kings, thieves, fire, water, and unloved heirs. In "training under him," the written རྗེས་ས is read as རྗེས་སུ.  "His wife will not be unfaithful" interprets ཆུང་མ་ལྷོད་པ་དང་བཅས་པ་མ་ཡིན་པ, literally "his wife will not be slack," that is, lax in fidelity. "Supreme celibate conduct" renders ཚངས་པར་སྤྱོད་པ (brahmacarya), the pure conduct of chastity, not the god Brahmā.  "Wherever he is born" reads གོང་ནས་གང་དུ as the stock phrase གང་ནས་གང་དུ found in the parallel sections. "Reconciliation" renders འདུམ་པ, the healing of discord that divisive speech destroys.  "Having the voice of Brahmā" renders ཚངས་པའི་དབྱངས་ཅན, the melodious Brahmā-like voice, here an attribute of the beings, not the goddess Sarasvatī.  "Idle talk" renders ཚིག་ཀྱལ་པ, frivolous or senseless speech, the seventh of the ten unwholesome actions.  The text reads ན་མ་འཚོའི་བར་དུ, evidently a slip for ནམ་འཚོའི་བར་དུ, "for as long as he lives," which is the reading in the parallel sentence that follows; translated accordingly. ཆགས་སེམས and འདོད་སེམས here name the eighth of the ten nonvirtues, covetousness (Skt. abhidhyā), so they are rendered "covetousness" rather than "attachment." རྒྱུད་ལྔ is the five destinies (gati) of saṃsāra, not "tantra." ཉེ་གནས (upasthāyaka or antevāsin) is rendered "disciples," referring to monastic pupils and attendants; "the disciples who are śrāvakas" renders ཉན་ཐོས་ཀྱི་ཉེ་གནས. The closing benefits of the eighth wheel repeat, almost word for word, those already given at the start of this piece for the seventh wheel; both are translated as they stand.  གནོད་སེམས is malice (Skt. vyāpāda), and ལོག་པར་ལྟ་བ wrong view (mithyādṛṣṭi), the ninth and tenth of the ten nonvirtues. In this piece བྱམས་པ means loving-kindness, listed beside compassion as an adornment of the buddha field; it is not the name Maitreya. བམ་པོ་དགུ་པ marks the beginning of the ninth section (bampo) of the Tibetan text.  ལྷའི་བདུད, rendered "Māra of the gods," is the divine Māra (devaputramāra), one of the four māras. ངོ་མཚར་དང་བཀྲ་ཤིས, rendered "belief in marvels and auspicious omens," appears to reflect the Sanskrit pair kautūhala and maṅgala, superstitious reliance on wonders and lucky signs. In the passage on the false claimant, "I will attain manifest awakening" supplies the sense of the first clause, which, like the two quoted claims, is governed by "says" (ཟེར་ན). དཀོན་མཆོག་གསུམ་གྱི་གདུང་བསབ་པ is taken as "to sustain the lineage of the Three Jewels."  The phrase བསྐལ་པར་བ་དག་ནི appears to be a copyist's slip; it is read as བསྐལ་པར་བདག་ཅག་ནི, "for eons ... 'We are followers of the Great Vehicle,'" which fits the sentence. མི་དགེ་བ་བཅུའི་སྡོམ་པ་ལ་མི་གནས་ན is taken as "if he does not remain committed to the ten nonvirtues," reflecting the Abhidharma notion of a binding commitment to wrongdoing. In the line introducing the verses, བཟླས་པ means "to restate," not mantra recitation. ཚངས་པའི་སྤྱད་པ is pure conduct (brahmacarya), not a reference to the god Brahmā, and བྱམས་པ in the verses is loving-kindness, not Maitreya.  ཤིན་ཏུ་རྒྱས་པ་ཆེན་པོ in the chapter colophon reflects Sanskrit mahāvaipulya, "great extensive [sūtra]." རྐང in the list of things given is marrow, not legs. གཞན་ལས་སྤག་གོ་སྙམ་པ is obscure; it is taken as the thought of being above others, that is, condescension toward the recipient, which fits the series of attitudes free of harshness, jealousy and miserliness. Though announced as ten, the wheels are not numbered in the text; they appear to correspond to the series of motives and attitudes in giving rather than to the list of things given.  "The indeterminate group" renders མ་ངེས་པའི་ཚོགས (aniyata-rāśi), the class of beings not yet fixed in either the right or the wrong direction, one of the three groups of beings. The line rendered "To persons of the two vehicles below" reads བར་རབ་ཐེག་པ་གཉིས་ཀྱི་གང་ཟག, whose wording is unclear; the sense, given the prose above, is that śrāvaka and pratyekabuddha practitioners may be taught the middle and highest vehicles. The name of the absorption, ཉི་མའི་སྒྲོན་མའི་འོད, is translated literally as "Light of the Sun Lamp" since no Sanskrit form could be confirmed. In this passage བཟླས་པ means "restate" and བགེགས means "obstruct" in their ordinary senses, not ritual ones.  The list of kinds of worldly patience is terse and some items are hard to construe; "patience that is not free of being troubled" renders ཇི་མི་སྙམ་པ་མེད་པའི་བཟོད་པ, and "patience of weak strength" and "patience that abandons sentient beings" divide the run མཐུ་ཞན་པའི་བཟོད་པའི་སེམས་ཅན་གཏོང་བའི་བཟོད་པ into two items. The sentence ending this piece is introduced by དེ་ལ་ཇི་ལྟར་ན and is rendered as a brief question followed by its answer.  "The three kinds of eon" renders བསྐལ་པ་རྣམ་གསུམ as it stands; in this list of afflictive factors it may stand for three kinds of conceptualization (vikalpa), but the text has been followed. The three bases of meritorious action (puṇyakriyāvastu) are giving, moral discipline and meditation.  In the first verse བུས་པ is read as བྱིས་པ, "fools" (literally "the childish"), since བུས་པ makes no sense here.  "The tenth and final fascicle" renders the section marker བམ་པོ་བཅུ་པ་སྟེ་ཐ་མ, which marks the start of the last fascicle (bam po) of the Tibetan translation. In the verses, "absorption" renders ཏིང་འཛིན (samādhi). The phrase ཤེས་རབ་དང་བཅས་པའི་ཤེས་རབ, "wisdom accompanied by the notion of wisdom", is terse; it seems to mean wisdom that still grasps at wisdom as an object.  Here མ་མོ, listed after the sūtras and the Vinaya, is mātṛkā in its sense of the Abhidharma matrices (the third basket), not the class of goddesses. The phrase "bring beings to full maturity through the means of attraction" reads བསྡུ་བའི་དངོས་པོ་རྣམས་ཞེས, where ཞེས appears to stand for ཀྱིས; translated accordingly. The phrase སངས་རྒྱས་གཞན་གྱིས་བཤད་པ་ལས་གཞན་པ is awkward; it is taken as "treatises other than those the Buddha taught" that nevertheless accord with liberation.  The opening sentence says the worldly skillful means is shared with śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, yet goes on to describe it as the cause on which the buddha qualities rest and as irreversible; the Tibetan gives no negative or contrast, so it is translated as it stands, though the second half may be meant to contrast with the shared kind.  Throughout this piece བྱམས་པ is the virtue of loving-kindness, not the name Maitreya. In the prose explanation the Tibetan says śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas devote themselves to "patience" (བཟོད་པ) focused on beings, where the argument leads one to expect "loving-kindness"; it is translated as written. "Solitary victors" renders རང་རྒྱལ, a verse form for pratyekabuddhas.  The second statement of the wheel's name reads "great steadfastness" (ཤིན་ཏུ་བརྟན་པ་ཆེན་པོ) where the first reads "very steadfast great patience" (ཤིན་ཏུ་བརྟན་པའི་བཟོད་པ་ཆེན་པོ); each is translated as written, though the same wheel is plainly meant.  "The Eye of Space and the Crown" renders ནམ་མཁའི་མིག and སྤྱི་བོ, which appear to be names of samādhis; their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence, so they are translated literally. In this piece བྱམས་པ is the virtue of loving-kindness, not the name Maitreya.  In the water simile the text reads ཞི་གནས ("calm abiding"), which makes no sense there; it is taken as a slip for a word meaning "destroyed" (perhaps ཞིག་ནས). The phrase ཆུ་འདིའི་ཁམས་ཆུང་ངུའི is also awkward and is rendered "of this small world". In the first wheel passage, what is "abandoned without remainder" is unstated; the parallels that follow suggest it is the karma leading to the lower realms.  ན་མ་འཚོའི་བར་དུ is read as ནམ་འཚོའི་བར་དུ "for as long as they lived," as in the parallel sentences that follow. The names of the two dhāraṇīs and the description of the patience (ཆོས་ལ་སྒྱུར་བ་འོད་སྣང་བ་ལས་རྟེན་པར་བྱེད་པ) are obscure; they are translated by meaning, and the rendering of the patience in particular is tentative.  The bodhisattva named རིགས་པར་ཐེ་ཙོམ་འདྲི་བ could not be identified with a known Sanskrit name and is translated by meaning. The Chinese preceptor's name is given in Tibetan phonetics, as the Tibetan records it.

It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
